How Does Diplo Survive a Weekend Running, DJing, and Partying? His Whoop Data Tells the Story.

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The 47-year-old DJ shared his health stats from a frenzied weekend that could’ve killed any normal person. Here’s how he does it without coming apart at the seams.

On a Saturday morning in August, at exactly 10 a.m., August Thomas Wesley Pentz, the DJ known as Diplo, stood outside the Barlume Beach Club in Montauk, Long Island and delivered a sermon to hundreds of runners. “I'm very tired; I slept three hours for you guys,” he shouted through a megaphone, dressed in grey cargo shorts, a tank top, and pink aviator shades. “But now we're ready to run. Let's have a good time.”

Then one of the world’s most prolific DJs launched himself into a 5K through the sand and rocks of Gin Beach. It was a run club event organized by A Team, a dating app for the wellness-obsessed, and Diplo's Run Club, which stages nine group runs across the U.S. (and soon, Europe).

As I watched him that sunny and hot morning, I couldn’t stop thinking: how the hell is this guy still standing, let alone leading a run? It was only Saturday, and Diplo was already deep into a frenzied weekend. He landed in New York City on Thursday morning after taking the overnight flight from his home in Malibu. That evening he DJ’d from 12:30 A.M. to 3 A.M. at the Edge, a venue 100 floors above the city. On Friday, he boarded a seaplane for the Hamptons, where he performed two more sets: a sunset show at Surf Lodge, where Jimmy Buffett and Snoop Dogg have played to crowds of models and billionaires, followed by an impromptu set at Mary Lou's, a Montauk club on the harbor where he’d only meant to hang out but ended up taking the decks. He didn't leave until around 1 a.m.

I was at both venues and felt deeply, existentially hungover the next morning. I figured—hoped, really—the run would include some walking. Diplo, however, led the pack. He wore a Whoop, the health tracker with which he has a partnership, and reached a heart rate of 167 beats per minute. “He wasn't taking it easy at all,” said Greg Grosicki, a staff research scientist at Whoop who reviewed Diplo’s data from his weekend in Montauk.

I didn't understand how a 47-year-old pulled off this feat—the travel, the DJ sets, the cavorting, the running—without collapsing and retreating to his hotel suite for the rest of the weekend. On Saturday evening he had two more sets, one at the annual gala of the Surfrider Foundation, which supports ocean conservation and includes Sean McPherson and John Slattery as supporters. After that he DJ’d a private party for investment executive Michael Dorrell before flying private to Ibiza overnight for his next gig.

After the run—which, miraculously, I did finish—I talked with Diplo about how he survives this lifestyle. He even shared his Whoop data with me from the weekend so I could compare his body’s reaction with how he told me he felt. “I'm actually fully cracked out every day, doing way more than running and DJing, more than anybody else,” he said.

He likes to sound nonchalant about it. “When I did an interview with some of the Whoop counselors, they were like, your body is very similar mentally to a Navy SEAL or an ER nurse. You're able to just get up and go do whatever,” he said. “I can always get up and get shit done, unless I'm sick.” OK, so maybe Diplo was just born with it. But I learned that he does more for his physical and mental health than he likes to let on. This is the story of how a 47-year-old, internationally renowned DJ, who works and parties into the pre-dawn hours, travels the world, and leads packs of people on runs, pulls it off without coming apart at the seams.

Running appeals to him because it travels well. All you need is a pair of shoes and the will to put them on. “I do things I can do anywhere,” he said. “Running is free.” He aims for 15 miles a week across three or four runs, averaging a seven-and-a-half to eight-minute mile. His fastest mile, six and a half minutes, came after his run club appeared on the prediction platform Polymarket, where people bet against him. “That's what motivated me to run pretty fast,” he said with a laugh.

DJing counts as cardio, too. After all, his sets typically run at least two hours. “It's a mix of dancing and standing,” he said. “It's almost like a little air squat.” He's baffled that his dancers, several from the group Major Lazer, stay in shape without a gym. “I'm like, ‘What do you guys do?’” he said. “They're like, ‘We don't do anything. We just dance at your shows.’"

The numbers back him up: during his Surf Lodge set, his max heart rate hit 117 bpm. “If you consider these sets are two hours long, maintaining a heart rate of 120 is a real physical activity,” Greg Grosicki, the research scientist at Whoop, told me. “It's equivalent to riding a bike for two hours. You feel pretty wrecked after that, and he's getting equivalent strain from his sets.”

Diplo mixes cardio and weights in HIIT sessions and does 100 to 200 push-ups a day, several times a week, usually in his hotel room. “It takes five minutes of your life,” he said.

Once a week he wears an electrical muscle stimulation, or EMS, suit for 20 minutes. “It does pulsing with simple moves,” he said, naming a brand called Catalyst and a newer one, Pepper, he's about to try. He credits the suit, half-jokingly, for his arm muscles.

He also takes hot Pilates classes with light weights and works out at Barry's Bootcamp near his home in Malibu. “There's an unwritten rule about group fitness in California: you don't bother celebrities,” he said. “You let them get their job done, then you tell people you saw Chris Martin. Every time I go to Pilates, he's next to me.”

According to the data, long flights, especially red eyes, hit Diplo hard, said Grosicki. After an overnight flight to Ibiza, Diplo's recovery score—a measure of how ready the body is to perform—dropped to 21 percent, and his sleep score to 29 percent. That’s well below average for him. “He's human, and he's responding to these stressors,” Grosicki said.

“I do like to get the red eyes, which probably messes up my sleep the most, but I'd rather be outside in the day,” Diplo said. “Being on a plane during the day is so depressing.” He said he struggles to hold a circadian rhythm and sometimes crashes mid-afternoon. On Thursday, for example, he slept five hours in the middle of the day ahead of his set at the Edge.

Winding down after a set is its own challenge. “I might take a Xanax if I have to,” he said. “I got to stop thinking about things.” He also puts his phone away, listens to audiobooks and history podcasts, skips food before bed (though he does chug electrolytes), and uses PEMF mats, which send low-frequency electromagnetic waves through the body to aid in relaxation. Mostly, though, he just takes what sleep he can get.

Saturday morning, after two Friday-night sets in Montauk, his recovery score was 67 percent and his sleep score 65 percent, both above average. On Monday, after he spent the night in Ibiza, his recovery score was 56 percent and sleep was 62 percent. As Grosicki put it, “He bounces back.”

Diplo avoids drinking before shows and on travel days and hasn't done recreational drugs in six months. When he does, it's acid. “I would run on acid, just try things on acid,” he said. “I could fly a plane on acid.”

“Mental health is pretty much everything,” Diplo said. “But you have to get there sometimes by exercising. Finding your body, being in tune with your body, is probably the first thing you can do to have a clear mind, to get rid of any sickness you have, whatever's keeping you down. That's the first way to start.”

He's tried more unconventional resets, too. Seven years ago, he smoked toad venom. “I'll never do it again, but it was pretty hardcore,” he said. “It's a really big ego death. Being humbled is very good wellness.”

Whatever he's doing, it seems to be working, at least by the numbers. Whether the rest of us should try it is a different question entirely.

AMD Stock Jumps 5% as Nvidia Loses Its CEO: Can AMD Capture the AI Opportunity?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AMD stock climbed 5.27% to $582.05 on November 12 as investors shifted attention toward Nvidia's semiconductor rivals.

Jensen Huang's departure from Nvidia has intensified interest in alternative AI chipmakers, including AMD and Broadcom.

AMD reported 97% annual growth in data-center revenue and forecast fourth-quarter revenue of approximately $17 billion.

Nvidia’s leadership crisis is giving AMD a fresh opportunity to attract Wall Street’s attention. AMD shares jumped 5.27% to $582.05 on November 12, extending their recovery as investors reassessed the competitive landscape following Jensen Huang’s departure from Nvidia. The shift comes at a critical time for AMD, which is already benefiting from accelerating demand for AI processors and server CPUs.

Can AMD stock turn Nvidia’s leadership setback into lasting market-share gains and push its stock above $650?

Nvidia’s sudden leadership change has created a new opening for its competitors, with AMD emerging as one of the immediate beneficiaries.

🚨BREAKING: Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO, effective immediately, ending 33 years leading the company he co-founded.$NVDA shares extend LOSSES to 10%.Nvidia announces:– Huang steps down as CEO, president and board member.– CFO Colette Kress becomes interim CEO while… pic.twitter.com/rf5wIon68U

Huang’s departure has raised questions about Nvidia’s ability to maintain its technology roadmap and customer relationships during the transition. CFO Colette Kress has taken over as interim CEO, but investors are still assessing the longer-term implications.

AMD and Broadcom shares have attracted buying interest as investors look beyond Nvidia for exposure to AI infrastructure growth. William Blair analyst Sebastien Naji also upgraded AMD to Outperform, citing Nvidia’s leadership uncertainty and Intel’s supply constraints as potential opportunities for AMD.

AMD’s investment case extends beyond Nvidia’s current difficulties. Its latest results show that demand for its data-center products is already accelerating. The company reported third-quarter revenue of $13.38 billion, up 45% year over year. Non-GAAP earnings per share increased 74% to $2.09, while data-center revenue grew 97%, driven by demand for Instinct GPUs and EPYC server processors.

Management expects fourth-quarter revenue of approximately $17 billion, plus or minus $300 million. CEO Lisa Su also expects overall revenue growth to exceed 80% in 2027. These figures strengthen AMD’s position in the AI infrastructure market. The next challenge is execution: converting rising demand into larger market share and sustained profitability.

AMD stock trades at $582.05 on November 12, gaining 5.27% during the session. The price chart shows a strong rebound after the stock briefly dropped toward $420–$450 earlier in November. Buyers have pushed shares back above the short- and medium-term moving averages near $510–$534, improving the near-term technical structure.

The immediate resistance zone sits between $650 and $680, where previous rallies encountered selling pressure. A sustained breakout above $680 could open the way toward $700. On the downside, $550 is the first support area, followed by $534 and $510. A decline below $510 would weaken the recovery and bring the longer-term support near $442 into focus.

Nvidia’s leadership transition has created an opportunity for AMD, but the semiconductor market will not change overnight. Customers must qualify alternative processors, adapt their software infrastructure and establish new supply arrangements before shifting large workloads.

AMD already has a growing data-center business, an expanding Instinct GPU portfolio and strong demand for EPYC processors. These fundamentals give the company a credible opportunity to compete for a larger share of AI infrastructure spending.

The real test for AMD is whether it can turn the current investor interest into lasting customer demand. A move above $650 would strengthen the bullish chart, but sustained revenue and market-share growth will ultimately determine the stock’s next leg.

Sociable: Meta’s AI ads included unapproved discounts

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Recent reports found that the artificial intelligence-powered ad tools posted discount offers to customers even with promotions turned off.

“Sociable” is the latest commentary on important social media developments and trends from industry expert Andrew Hutchinson of Social Media Today.

As artificial intelligence tools become more empowered, new parameters need to be established to limit their capacity when it comes to undertaking certain tasks.

There have already been reports of an AI tool giving out a user’s home address, generating hundreds of videos at a users’ expense and conducting questionable crypto trades.

Automated errors can also extend to buying digital ads. PPC Land reported last week that Meta’s AI-powered ad tools took discounts from a store’s website and displayed them on the business’s Facebook and Instagram ads, “even when the shop had turned promotions off on the ad it copied.”

The discovery was made by digital marketing advisor Bram Van der Hallen, who shared a LinkedIn post outlining the discovery.

As per Van der Hallen: “Meta may now automatically detect offers from your website and use them in your Meta ads. Even when you have turned off this option for your original ad. When the option to display offers detected from your website and ads is enabled in Commerce Manager, Meta may then automatically display these offers in your ads.”

Van der Hallen said the issue can be controlled by updating Commerce Manager settings to stop Meta from using detected offers in other campaigns.

But this adds another element to consider, and PPC Land noted that it could cause issues for advertisers: “This matters for any business advertising on Meta with more than one offer live, because a discount meant for one campaign or one audience could show up somewhere else.”

Meta recently outlined how its expanded processing capacity is helping generate more effective ad campaigns. The company said that improvements to its ads ranking system drove a more than 8% increase in ad clicks and a 15.7% uplift in conversions on Facebook in Q2 2026.

However, as more systems are guided by AI, there will be errors because AI systems seek out efficiencies and optimizations wherever they can, which could cause issues.

Advertisers need to be aware of these potential issues, which could have a big impact on campaign approaches.

Genshin Impact cheater offering paid carry services reportedly arrested while meeting with customers

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A Genshin Impact player in China has reportedly been arrested after allegedly using cheats to help customers complete difficult in-game challenges in exchange for money.

The player is accused of running a paid boosting service for Stygian Onslaught, one of Genshin Impact’s toughest combat challenges, using unauthorized tools to clear content for other players.

However, the alleged operation came to an abrupt end when police reportedly detained the individual during a meeting with customers inside a clothing store.

According to Enduins, the player had been advertising their services through videos on the Chinese platform Bilibili, demonstrating how cheats could be used to complete Stygian Onslaught.

The challenge requires players to complete three timed battles, with higher difficulties offering significantly tougher encounters.

The allegations began circulating on Chinese gaming forum NGA before being shared by Seele Leaks on Telegram, alongside images documenting the incident.

One image appears to show several individuals gathered inside a clothing store in security camera footage dated November 3, 2026. Another shows a Chinese-language apology statement displayed on a phone.

The player subsequently apologized to HoYoverse and removed posts advertising their services.

“I used this account without authorization to publicly post videos of myself using cheats in Genshin Impact. I then directed viewers from Bilibili to my Xianyu secondhand marketplace store where I offered paid game boosting services,” they wrote.

“My actions seriously violated miHoYo’s and Genshin Impact’s official rules prohibiting cheating. They infringed upon miHoYo’s intellectual property rights and disrupted the game’s operations, fairness, and the gaming experience of other players.”

Genshin Impact devs vow legal action against cheaters ruining game

Genshin Impact players review bomb HoYoverse office on Google Maps over reward backlash

Genshin Impact “secretly” sent currency to streamers to get characters

This wouldn’t be the first time HoYoverse has taken serious action against cheating. In March 2026, the developer revealed that it had removed more than one million illegal links and pieces of content during 2025, including cheat software and pirated game resources.

The company also previously threatened legal action against Genshin Impact cheaters in 2023 after malicious plug-ins were used to interfere with other players’ accounts.

China has also seen legal consequences for paid boosting operations. In September 2025, a Shanghai court ordered a Genshin Impact boosting studio to pay 3 million yuan in damages after finding that it had used cheats to provide services to customers.

For the latest alleged offender, what started as a way to make money clearing Genshin Impact challenges may now have become a much more serious legal matter.

Grace and Bryan—two peas in a very scared pod.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Resident Evil Requiem's director, Koshi Nakanishi, once said that putting Leon Kennedy in a horror game simply wouldn't work because "he wouldn't jump at something like a bucket falling. No one wants to see Leon scared by everything". And he was right, I couldn't have felt safer with Leon in Requiem. Grace on the other hand, we were decidedly less safe in her hands.

But that didn't mean I enjoyed my time with her any less. While Leon's segments in Requiem were pure unbridled feel-good action, Grace found herself in one exhilaratingly stressful situation after another, not unlike another Resident Evil protagonist who rocked up this year. I'm of course talking about Bryan Hodukavich from Zach Cregger's Resident Evil movie.

One of my favourite parts of the Resident Evil film was just how ordinary Bryan was, it not only made it infinitely scarier (especially the first part) but I felt empathy for this poor delivery driver in a way that I probably wouldn't for a trained bioweapon killing machine.

I hadn't actually thought about how alike Grace and Bryan are in this way, not until Grace's actress Angela Sant'Albano pointed it out herself during an interview with Radio Times Gaming.

"I thought the film was amazing," Sant'Albano says. "I'm a big Zach Cregger fan, I loved Weapons. I think what he's done is really brilliant and also, you know, their lead character is not Grace, but he is sort of an everyday person put in this extraordinary circumstance and I just think that really translates well to audiences. They want to feel like it could be them. So I'm a big fan of [Cregger] and always have been, and a big fan of the film."

It's certainly hard to be a fan of the games and not love Cregger's Resident Evil adaptation. I honestly don't think I've seen a better videogame adaptation, perhaps Cyberpunk: Edgerunners, but it's a close call. The only thing that would make Cregger's adaptation better in my books is if we somehow got to see Bryan cross paths with one of the videogame protagonists, but sadly that simply isn't possible—for many reasons.

Apple says it will shut down its Apple Support Community forums after 28 years

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you've ever had a problem with your MacBook, iPhone, or Apple account, a quick Google search for a fix likely sent you to a relevant thread on the Apple Support Community at discussions.apple.com, where Apple customers tried to help their fellow Apple fans out.

On Tuesday, Apple announced that it would be closing down the Apple Support Community, the official forum for Apple customers looking for help from other Apple users with their Apple products.

Apple disabled posting in the Apple Support Community on Tuesday. The platform is still viewable in a read-only state. However, the forums will be shuttered for good on Nov. 20.

A prompt now appears at the top of the Apple Support Community website and all of its posts with Apple's announcement of the forum's closure: The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

"The Apple Support Community will permanently close on November 20, 2026. Starting today, the site will no longer accept new posts and is available in a read-only state.

You can continue to find trusted guidance and support on the official Apple Support site.

We want to thank the community members who have contributed their time and expertise to help others with their Apple products."

Anyone who has ever had an Apple product likely found themselves on the Apple Support Community at some point. Even if they didn't sign up to ask a question themselves, the answer to their question likely existed in the Apple Support Community archives, helping numerous customers avoid reaching out directly to Apple's official support.

The forum launched in 1998 as the Apple Tech Exchange. According to MacRumors, the forums went through numerous name changes over the years, going by Apple Discussion in 2000, before the company finally settled on the Apple Support Community in 2022.

The last post on the Apple Support Community appears to have been published by a user looking for help with their Apple account at 5:41 p.m. on Nov. 10. One last reply was posted in the thread minutes later at 5:49 p.m. Apple appears to have moved the forums to a read-only state shortly after that.

Apple reportedly told MacRumors that "it decided to sunset the Apple Support Community after a careful evaluation of strategy and customer needs."

What if you need help with your Apple products going forward? Apple suggests that you contact its official support directly.

Mike O'Malley opened up about his former co-star.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Actor Anthony Clark, star of the early 2000s CBS sitcom Yes, Dear, has passed away. Co-star and actor Mike O'Malley shared the news, as he paid tribute to Clark and talked about the friendship they had before they ever worked together on television. He also opened up about the last phone call he had with the late actor.

O'Malley, who recently appeared on the 2026 TV schedule via a guest spot on NCIS, said a few words about Clark on X. He made audiences laugh as Greg, who often played the straight man to the sillier antics of his friend Jimmy. O'Malley talked about how he first met Clark, and how they'd only just recently spoken before his death:

Anthony Clark died yesterday. He was a hilarious guy. When my wife Lisa and I were dating back in the mid-nineties, Anthony was already famous in Boston, regularly selling out comedy shows. The first time we saw him at The Comedy Connection we laughed and laughed and laughed. He was so unique—one of those comics who is laughing along with you at the absurdity of his stories and observations. I was a fan before I was a friend. We spoke on the phone three weeks ago. He had us laughing loudly once again. Attached are some snapshots from our last episode of 'Yes Dear' which ended twenty years ago after 122 episodes. Rest in peace. Tony.

While Yes, Dear isn't available with a Paramount+ subscription or on other platforms, it was a successful CBS comedy in its day, with a six-season run. Greg and Jimmy's families represented two different households and the vastly different ways they raised their children, despite their long friendship.

32 Underrated TV Shows From The '90s That Don't Get Talked About Much These Days

Mike O'Malley has continued his acting career with more appearances on sitcoms, including memorable stints on My Name Is Earl and Glee. As for Anthony Clark, he had a brief hosting stint on Last Comic Standing, but ultimately stepped away from Hollywood and lived a quiet life.

As for the cause of death, Deadline reported that Anthony Clark passed following complications from pneumonia. O'Malley did not make mention of the illness in his tribute, and it is not known how long the former actor struggled from the sickness.

While Clark was known to many as a television star, his start came in stand-up comedy. He was famously featured in a 1995 HBO special in which he starred alongside Dave Chappelle, Dave Attell, and Louis C.K.

After the special, he secured a few film roles, including the barber seen in Sean Connery's The Rock. I was also reminded while looking at his filmography that he was in the drama Paid in Full, as well as River Phoenix's The Thing Called Love. His final film appearance would be in the 2011 comedy My Uncle Rafael, which starred John Michael Higgins.

He had a recurring guest role on Ellen, too, which ultimately paved the way for his leading role on Yes, Dear. I'm a little shocked to see it isn't available on streaming, and I would love to check out one of the best sitcoms of the 2000s again in light of this news.

CinemaBlend offers its condolences to the family and friends of Anthony Clark, and wishes them well during this time. Here's hoping for more tributes from those around Hollywood and stand-up comedy who knew him, and more stories about his life.

Anthropic’s IPO is reportedly oversubscribed as founders sell $1.75bn

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic’s seven founders are selling $1.75bn of stock, and Amazon and Google stand to gain hundreds of billions. Retail investors in Europe, Australia and Japan can join the Anthropic IPO.

Anthropic’s share sale has drawn orders for several times the shares on offer, Bloomberg reported on 11 November, citing people familiar with the matter. Large, anchor-size orders drove the demand, the people said.

The day before, the Financial Times reported that bankers were preparing to raise the valuation to $1.5tn or more. That would top the $1.4tn ceiling Anthropic set on 10 November. Details of the deal could still change, Bloomberg’s sources said.

The deal is set to price on 18 November, Bloomberg’s Bailey Lipschultz reported. Before that, Dario Amodei is due to join JPMorgan chief executive Jamie Dimon for a livestreamed event on Friday, according to Bloomberg.

Some early investors are already reselling. JonesTrading is marketing a block of up to $500m at a fee of 3% to 5% over the IPO price, Bloomberg reported. Anthropic says it does not recognise share transfers it has not approved.

Anthropic first disclosed its third-quarter loss on 2 November. Its amended filing of 10 November adds more detail on the quarter.

Anthropic put the move from a second-quarter operating profit down to two costs. They were $5bn of short-term compute and a $2bn charge for its staff donation-matching programme. Price cuts on new models cost about $600m in revenue between the two quarters. Adjusted operating margin fell to 15% from 19%.

The filing also says that 5% to 7% of revenue in the first nine months came from customers later found to have broken its usage policy or commercial terms. Anthropic tightened enforcement in August and September and expects that to weigh on growth.

Anthropic held $95.6bn in cash and $36.3bn in short-term investments, the filing shows. The Information’s Aaron Holmes reported about $10bn of new cloud commitments with Microsoft and Google since the last filing. The filing also says the $35bn Lambda deal can currently be ended, though Anthropic has no intention of doing so.

The seven co-founders are selling 23,648,646 shares between them, according to Anthropic’s roadshow announcement. That is 3,378,378 each, or about $250m apiece at the $74 midpoint, $1.75bn in total.

The sale is about 1.5% of each founder’s stake, Dylan Sloan reported for Bloomberg. Each would be worth about $18bn at the midpoint, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index. All seven have pledged to give away 80% of their personal equity.

“The market has gotten more comfortable with founders and large shareholders taking some money off the table,” Jay Ritter, an emeritus professor at the University of Florida, told Bloomberg.

Amazon and Google, Anthropic’s largest outside shareholders, together own more than a quarter of its shares, Karen Weise reported for The New York Times. At $1.4tn, Amazon’s $18bn investment could be worth more than $250bn, and Google’s $33bn about $140bn.

“It is a loaded bet, but I don’t think anybody wants to be left out of the bet,” Aswath Damodaran, who teaches valuation at New York University, told the Times.

Nvidia is putting about $10bn into the private placement. Its chief executive, Jensen Huang, resigned on Thursday, and Nvidia reports earnings on 17 November.

Anthropic is selling to retail investors in several countries without listing outside the US. Its European offer covers Denmark, Finland, France, Germany, Ireland, the Netherlands, Norway, Spain and Sweden. Germany’s financial regulator, BaFin, approved the prospectus, which also covers Swiss retail investors.

Australia’s retail offer opened on 11 November and closes at 5pm Sydney time on 17 November, CNBC reported, citing broker Stake. Commonwealth Bank’s CommSec leads a group of eight brokers.

In Japan, Anthropic plans to sell 38.5m to 50m shares, worth $3bn to $3.5bn, Bloomberg reported. The filing also covers Canada and the UK.

Craig Coben, a former head of equity capital markets at Bank of America, told The New York Times that everyone has an interest in the deal’s success.

“A successful I.P.O. is arguably required to validate the valuations of stocks in the A.I. ecosystem,” Coben said.

Jack Selby, who runs the venture firm Copper Sky, warned of the opposite on Bloomberg Television.

“An underperforming Anthropic IPO has to be a very prominent player in what will be a pretty massive correction in the market with respect to AI,” Selby said.

He also pointed to the end of lock-up periods, when early holders can sell. Up to 316m shares can trade from the first day, the filing shows.

Every Show in Marvel’s WandaVision Trilogy, Ranked

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

VisionQuest has finally aired its series finale, bringing closure to the unexpected trilogy that began in 2021 with WandaVision and continued in 2024 with the even more unexpected Agatha All Along. At face value, the saga is a tale about how a witch and a synthezoid deal with the loss of their makeshift family, set within the confines of a superhero cinematic universe. In reality, the WandaVision trilogy is about two main things: the first is grief, how we process it, what it does to us, and how we eventually learn to live with it. The second is family. It's a story about grieving mothers, struggling fathers, and lost children, all coming together to find purpose and belonging.

It wouldn't be an overstatement to say the WandaVision trilogy is the single best, most cohesive and narratively impactful thing the MCU has ever produced. For starters, every show prioritizes meaning over spectacle and character over action; the result is a tale of multiple wandering characters who connect through shared experience, learn to process their pain, and ultimately gain new insight about the world and themselves, entering the next chapter of their lives as updated versions of themselves. Declaring which of them is the best is not an easy task, but someone has to do it. Here, we rank every entry in the WandaVision trilogy, not as a way to bring them down, but to celebrate what is undoubtedly Marvel's boldest, most compelling and cathartic creative swing.

The trilogy began in 2021 with WandaVision, written and created by Jac Schaeffer. Set in the aftermath of Avengers: Endgame, the show sees Wanda Maximoff (Elizabeth Olsen) create an idealized version of her life inspired by her love for classic sitcoms. In this world, Vision (Paul Bettany) is alive, and they even welcome twins. Of course, Wanda's life is complicated by the presence of S.W.O.R.D. and a mysterious nosy neighbor named Agnes (Kathryn Hahn). Oh, and there's also the fact that she enslaved an entire town to create this pretty little life, so there's that.

WandaVision is, first and foremost, a character study on Wanda Maximoff, and Elizabeth Olsen makes the most of it, delivering what might just be the best performance in the MCU. The writing is sharp and insightful, using Wanda's trauma to continue her character arc and keep the larger MCU storyline going. Bettany and Hahn are excellent support, as is Teyonah Parris as Monica Rambeau. The show focuses on the destructive power of grief, but also its ability to change and help us grow, finding a perfect way to include the superhero aspect of the story. Alas, WandaVision is not perfect, and it's pretty much undone by its rushed, borderline unsatisfying finale. The subsequent Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness also did a disservice to the show by pretty much committing character assassination with Wanda, which certainly doesn't help.

The most recent effort, VisionQuest, is also the last chapter in the trilogy, and it does a wonderful job tying everything together. Set about 18 months after the events of Agatha All Along, the show centers on Vision as he settles into his new reality with the memories from the old Vision. Several AIs are also present in his programming, including E.D.I.T.H. (Emily Hampshire), F.R.I.D.A.Y. (Orla Brady), J.A.R.V.I.S. (James D'Arcy), and Ultron (James Spader). Vision's life is put in danger when a bounty hunter is sent after him, causing him to reunite with his lost son, Tommy (Ruaridh Mollica).

VisionQuest is a pretty perfect finale to the story, putting the spotlight on fathers and their complicated relationships with their sons. The Vision-Tommy bond is great, but the real center of the story is the Vision-Ultron father-son dynamic, with Bettany and Spader not only sharing excellent chemistry but also making the most of an unexpectedly powerful screenplay. Spader, in particular, is very clearly having the time of his life here, bringing an electrifying energy to the show. The AIs are a worthy addition, too, even if they're used a bit too much like a deus ex machina that benefits from using a lot of technical jargon that may or may not make actual sense. Yet, at its core, VisionQuest is a tale of self-discovery and coming to terms with one's role in life as a father, brother, and son. It continues the family angle while finally giving the spotlight to Vision, one of the MCU's most reliable yet sidelined characters.

Who would've thought the show that was seemingly inspired by a catchy tune would be the best in this trilogy? Alas, that's precisely the case. Agatha All Along picks up two years after WandaVision, with Agatha Harkness (Hahn) breaking out of Wanda's spell with the help of a young Teen (Joe Locke) who wants her help to walk the Witches' Road. Recruiting a coven, the duo ventures onto the Road, followed by the Salem Seven, a group of witches hell-bent on revenge against Agatha. The mysterious Rio Vidal (Aubrey Plaza) also has unfinished business with Agatha.

Agatha All Along is the most cohesive, well-planned, and best-executed series, not only in the WandaVision trilogy but in the MCU as a whole. What might seem like a gag show is actually a very tender and thoughtful exploration of motherhood and grief from the perspective of the MCU's most volatile antihero. Agatha is evil, selfish, and ruthless, a killer through and through, but she's also unexpectedly loving, and her bond with the Teen is as sweet as it's hilarious. Speaking of Teen, Locke is a revelation as Billy Maximoff, bringing a lot of nuance and plenty of sass to his performance. Narratively, Agatha All Along takes bold swings, centering its entire story on a song and delivering some of Marvel's most interesting episodes, most notably the endlessly creative "Death's Hand in Mine." With a terrific ensemble including heavy-hitters like Patti LuPone, a charming visual approach that has a high school play vibe (the Road is very much a high school play of sorts), and a thematic richness seldom found in other superhero properties, Agatha All Along is truly one of a kind.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Doctors push back on Amodei’s claim AI will end cancer within a decade

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Germany’s former health minister says AI cannot cure cancer in ten years, and doctors push back. Anthropic’s founders say cures are within reach, and point to work with the WHO.

Dario Amodei, chief executive of Anthropic, at Fortune Brainstorm Tech in Park City, Utah, in July 2023.

Germany’s former health minister Karl Lauterbach has said that curing cancer, Alzheimer’s and mental illness within ten years is impossible. He was answering Dario Amodei, whose company’s IPO roadshow video says those diseases will be “relics of the past” within a decade.

Doctors and a biotech executive have pushed back too, Business Insider reported on 11 November. Truman Dickerson gathered the reactions as Anthropic courts investors ahead of its listing.

Anthropic began marketing its shares on 10 November with a roadshow video. In it, Amodei, the company’s chief executive, named four diseases.

“You know, if today we look at things like cancer, Alzheimer’s, heart disease, mental illness, these will be relics of the past in, I think, less than a decade,” Amodei said.

“I think if we do this right, we can create an age of miracles where humanity is able to solve problems that have eluded us for millennia,” he added.

He tied the promise to safety. The company that gets the risks right will have “the license to operate that society trusts,” he said.

The seven co-founders made the medical case in their letter to investors, first published with the original IPO filing. AI could “bring cures for major diseases within reach,” they wrote.

“First, we believe that AI will profoundly accelerate the rate of progress in biology and medicine, potentially curing diseases that have plagued humanity for millennia, including cancer, Alzheimer’s disease, heart disease, rare genetic diseases, and the major infectious diseases,” the letter says.

Anthropic already works with pharmaceutical companies on tasks from drug discovery to clinical trials, the letter says. It says its work can cut the time to write clinical trial reports “from months to minutes.”

The letter gives other examples. The World Health Organization’s Regional Office for Africa used Claude during the 2026 Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. Preparing its daily situation report fell from a full day to under an hour, the founders wrote.

With the Gates Foundation, Anthropic supports free AI clinical search for doctors in roughly 100 low- and middle-income countries. The founders also wrote that Anthropic has discovered a molecular machine that might become a new biotechnology tool, perhaps for gene editing.

About 4.6 billion people still lack access to essential health services, the letter says, citing the WHO and the World Bank. AI can help get treatments to where they are most needed, the founders wrote.

The same letter lists “underpromise and overdeliver” among Anthropic’s guiding principles.

Lauterbach, a doctor and epidemiologist, answered in German on X on 11 November.

“It is unclear whether people like @DarioAmodei know so little about medicine or are deliberately misleading the public. Curing cancer, Alzheimer’s and mental illness can definitely benefit from AI. But it cannot possibly happen in just 10 years,” Lauterbach wrote (translated by TNW).

He added that the focus should be on prevention.

Melinda B. Chu, an AI medical researcher and doctor at Saint Louis University, also questioned the claim, according to Business Insider.

“Anthropic has done nothing to suggest they themselves will contribute meaningfully to these goals,” Chu said.

Rami Elghandour, a biotech executive, pointed to the setting of the claim.

“I’ve taken two companies public. These are insane things to say in an IPO pitch,” Elghandour said, according to Business Insider.

Amodei has questioned this kind of promise himself. In an X post in August, he wrote that “saying that AI will cure cancer is more a cliché than it is inspiring, and most people think it is deceptive.” The best way to win over sceptics was to “actually cure cancer,” he wrote, as Business Insider noted.

Anthropic has started building its own biology work. In October, it bought a one-person biotech start-up working on muscular dystrophy. It has bought several biotech start-ups, according to Business Insider.

Anthropic said in an October blog post that it had “begun drug discovery efforts of our own,” Business Insider noted. It also reported, citing a person familiar, that Anthropic runs a wet lab in the Bay Area.

The letter says Anthropic will work with partners to validate the discoveries it expects Claude to make. Its public benefit fund of at least 5% of its shares could also go to science, medicine and global health, the founders wrote.

Anthropic’s IPO seeks a $1.4 trillion valuation. Its shares are due to start trading on Nasdaq on 19 November.

Nvidia live updates: Jensen Huang is out as CEO

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jensen Huang officially resigned as Nvidia CEO on Thursday morning, a seismic event in the tech industry.

Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies in the world, with a nearly $4 trillion valuation, and Huang was the company's original CEO and co-founder. The company's board of directors shared a CEO transition plan on its website, saying Huang has also resigned as president and as a member of the board, effective immediately.

The company's CFO, Colette Kress, will serve as interim CEO, while also maintaining her CFO responsibilities. The board has begun searching for a new CEO, looking both inside and outside the company.

The tech industry has been closely watching Nvidia and its stock ever since news first broke by the New York Times that Huang had not one, but two affairs, including one with an accused Chinese spy. Nvidia chips provide much of the compute that powers the fast-growing AI industry, and so Huang's alleged entanglement with a possible Chinese spy wasn't just a typical executive PR scandal, but a national security issue.

The story is still developing, so keep checking back at Mashable for the latest Nvidia news and live updates.

Last Friday, New York Times and Financial Times reported dropped a bomb on the tech industry. The publications reported that Huang had an affair with a woman the U.S. intelligence community believed to be a Chinese spy. China disputed these accusations, of course, but the Times reported that Secretary of State Marco Rubio personally wrote a memo alerting President Donald Trump to the situation in the spring.

On Nov. 8, Huang posted a personal message to the Nvidia website, admitting to and apologizing for the affair.

"I owe my family and all of you an apology," Huang wrote. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry."

It was unclear whether Huang would be able to hold on to his position as CEO, and the Nvidia board of directors announced it had opened an investigation into the incident.

But on Wednesday, news of a second affair may have sealed Huang's fate. The New York Times reported that Huang had another extramarital affair with another Nvidia executive who reported to him. The Times identified that executive as Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare.

This is a developing story. The latest Nvidia news will be posted live on this page...

United Airlines blocks AI agents from booking flights, citing cybersecurity risks

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

United Airlines said it will restrict access to AI agents seeking to book flights and manage reservations on their users’ behalf, citing a risk to cybersecurity and undermining federal regulations.

The airline is the latest American corporate giant to prevent customers from using AI agents and autonomous assistants to access their systems. The move comes as airlines and banks are struggling to differentiate between real customers and AI systems operating on their behalf, such as when users share their logins with their AI assistants. Sharing passwords with apps and assistants can create security risks if those credentials get leaked or exposed.

United warns that “sharing account credentials with personal, third-party, AI assistants can expose sensitive information, and actions taken by these tools may result in unintended purchases, account changes, or use of MileagePlus miles.”

The airline also warns of a risk of violating federal aviation rules, such as requiring that a human completes flight check-ins to acknowledge that their bags do not contain banned substances.

“As a result, United is taking steps to restrict access — directly within our digital platforms and indirectly via travel agencies and aggregators — to prohibit use by unauthorized, third-party personal AI agents or assistants,” the airline said in a statement. “This work includes updates to our booking, ticketing and distribution policies.”

The airline’s move follows a warning from banks, including Bank of America, which last week urged customers not to share their passwords with their AI assistants.

Google and Samsung’s Gemini smart glasses go on preorder from $395

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nine styles from Warby Parker and Gentle Monster ship on 10 December, taking on Meta's Ray-Bans.

Gentle Monster’s smart glasses, made with Google and Samsung, cost $449 to $489 and ship on 10 December.

Google and Samsung have opened preorders for their first Gemini smart glasses, made with Warby Parker and Gentle Monster. The nine styles cost $395 to $489, have a 12-megapixel camera and ship on 10 December. Google is stressing privacy features as it takes on Meta.

Google and Samsung have opened preorders for their first Gemini-powered smart glasses, Bloomberg reported. The glasses, made with eyewear brands Warby Parker and Gentle Monster, ship on 10 December.

There are nine styles. Gentle Monster’s six cost $449 to $489, while Warby Parker’s three start at $395. Buyers order through the two brands’ websites and stores, and some Google and Samsung stores will offer demos.

All styles weigh around 41 grams and last up to nine hours on a charge, Google said in a blog post. They run on a Qualcomm Snapdragon AR1 chip, have a 12-megapixel camera and work with iPhones and some Pixel and Galaxy phones. Warby Parker’s models launch only in the US, while Gentle Monster’s also go on sale in South Korea, Samsung said.

Users can say “Hey Gemini” to ask about what they are looking at, add a flyer to their calendar or get recipe ideas from the food in front of them. The glasses also offer walking directions, live translation, music and calls.

Google is leaning on privacy. The camera stops working if its recording light is covered, and bystanders’ faces are blurred before Gemini sees an image. Users must be 18 or over and agree not to film people without permission, or their glasses can be disabled.

“As glasses become more capable, our focus is on creating helpful experiences that make both wearers and bystanders feel protected,” said Shahram Izadi, who leads Google’s XR unit.

Google first showed the glasses at I/O in May, and Samsung showed them again in July. They now face Meta, which shipped 81% of AI glasses in the first half of the year and sells own-brand glasses from $299.

Meta’s own recording light has been beaten by a $2 sticker, and Google has banned such accessories from its shopping and ad platforms.

Can we please stop calling it a ‘honey trap’?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When Jensen Huang told Nvidia employees on November 8 that he had been “unfaithful” to his wife, there’s a phrase that quickly became omnipresent in the media: Huang had been the victim of a “honey trap”, laid by Alice Huang (no relation), a Singapore-based tech executive identified as a Chinese spy by U.S. intelligence agencies.

“Honey trap,” seriously?

The story behind the phrase is that a good, hardworking man was minding his own business when a serpent of a woman lured him off the righteous path by dishonest means and propositions he, being a man, simply couldn’t refuse. It is assumed that male sex drive is some kind of force of nature that no adult can reasonably be expected to control. So there’s a double sexist stereotype here. Can a 63-year-old billionaire CEO really be supposed to have no say over his sex life? Like a bear wouldn’t be able to resist honey?

Anyone who, like me, grew up in the 1980s knows a close relative of this idea: the “homewrecker,” a word aimed at the other woman and never at the husband who cheated. One of the decade’s most successful films, Fatal Attraction (1987), tells the story of a married man (Michael Douglas) whose affair turns into a nightmare when the woman (Glenn Close) refuses to stay discreet and starts asking for more. The film portrays the mistress as mentally unstable and dangerous. The film ends with the wife killing the mistress and family values restored. The husband’s betrayal is never really an issue. I’m ashamed to admit that when I first watched it, some time in the 1990s, I sided completely with the poor husband, hounded by a woman the film had led me to see as a witch.

Both ideas have their roots in Genesis, where Adam, asked why he ate the forbidden fruit, answers that the woman gave it to him. It is easy scapegoating: powerful men keep their reputation for rationality while some snake of a woman takes the blame for their lapses of judgment. There’s a paradox that I find difficult to swallow. For centuries, women were judged too foolish to vote or hold office, yet the same culture credits them with enough cunning to bring down kings and CEOs. Men, meanwhile, are supposed to be fit to lead nations and markets while being too weak to resist the first temptation that comes their way.

Deadly clichés

The term comes from spy fiction: dictionaries credit John le Carré with popularizing “honey trap” in Tinker Tailor Soldier Spy in 1974. Espionage has long been a man’s world, and in its novels a female spy is very often a seductress. Real Cold War espionage looked quite different, since East Germany’s best-known seduction program used men, so-called “Romeo” agents sent west to seduce secretaries with access to government files. 

In the Nvidia story, much of the coverage of Alice Huang also draws on images Western culture has attached to Asian women for more than a century: the mysterious seductress, the Dragon Lady. In the U.S., they go back at least to the Page Act of 1875, the country’s first restrictive federal immigration law, which was enforced on the presumption that Chinese women were prostitutes. Any woman arriving from Asia had to prove she wasn’t one.

In March 2021, a white man killed eight people at three Atlanta-area spas, six of them Asian women, and police relayed his description of his victims as “sources of temptation.” Asian women end up being blamed for men’s desire. And Atlanta showed that this cliché can be deadly. Coverage casting Alice Huang as a seductress is anything but harmless.

Nobody trapped the CEO

A CEO’s sexual relationship with someone working for one of his company’s biggest customers is a clear conflict of interest. Alice Huang’s employer, Megaspeed, is said to have bought about $4.8 billion of Nvidia hardware over the past two years. In most companies, a sales manager sleeping with a key client’s buyer would be expected to disclose it and hand the account to a colleague. Nobody would describe that sales manager as “trapped,” and the CEO of one of the world’s most valuable companies, who has far more power, deserves no more indulgence.

The phrase also shrinks Alice Huang: if she is only “honey,” she cannot be much of a professional. Yet she ran a business that bought chips at enormous scale, and if the allegations hold, she will have to answer for export violations and espionage, serious crimes committed with the very agency the word denies her.

Call it what it is

The honey trap trope has consequences in the workplace. In 2019, a LeanIn.Org and SurveyMonkey survey found that 60% of male managers in the U.S. were uncomfortable mentoring, working one-on-one with, or socializing with a woman, and 36% of men said they had avoided doing so for fear of how it would look. A woman alone with a powerful man is seen as a danger to him, even though she is far more likely to be the one harassed. She also pays in lost dinners, business trips and sponsorship, the informal moments that decide who gets promoted.

Other words would be more honest. A CEO had an affair with an executive at one of his largest customers, a woman U.S. officials reportedly believe was working for Chinese intelligence, and his board is now examining whether the relationship compromised the company. It is less catchy than “honey trap,” but it treats both people as adults responsible for their choices. Isn’t that the standard any employee would expect?

Anthropic says it has met its own threshold for AI-accelerated research

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic’s latest IPO filing bases its AI research threshold call on what it expects from future models. METR, given rare internal access, estimates its models are improving about twice as fast as before.

Anthropic has formally decided that it meets its own threshold for AI dramatically speeding up its research. It based the call on “what we expect from future models and our overall rate of progress,” not on its current models, it wrote in its amended IPO filing on 10 November.

The wording hardened over three filings in 15 days, as Alex Wilhelm noted in his newsletter Cautious Optimism. The AI research threshold sits in Anthropic’s Responsible Scaling Policy, the company’s own rulebook for when stronger safeguards apply.

The policy counts a “dramatic acceleration” as double the rate of progress seen without significant help from AI. It also requires that AI automating research could plausibly explain the doubling.

The first S-1, filed on 26 October, said Anthropic’s evidence on AI-driven acceleration came mostly from lagging indicators.

“As a result, we may not identify a significant acceleration in our rate of development until after it has occurred,” the filing said.

The first amendment followed on 2 November. Anthropic wrote that it had “in the past not identified” such a speed-up until afterwards. Its estimates for future models showed it “may imminently meet” the threshold, it added. It had not designated any current model as exceeding it.

Three days later, the evaluation group METR published an independent assessment of Anthropic’s research pace. Its staff worked on Anthropic devices from 12 August to 29 October, with what METR called “unprecedented access” to internal data.

METR found that Anthropic’s models were improving on well-specified tasks at around twice the rate of March 2024 to October 2025. Its central estimate was 1.9 times, within a range of 1.5 to 2.8.

“We guess that a majority (perhaps ≈80%) of the current (October 2026) acceleration is due to AI,” METR wrote.

METR also found that AI cannot yet fully automate substantial parts of Anthropic’s model training pipeline. Still, it saw “a significant risk that further capabilities progress could lead to even faster acceleration over the coming year.”

The report carried caveats. The speed-up might be limited to well-specified tasks, METR said. Anthropic also held redaction rights over all non-public information in the report.

“Anthropic redacted a majority of the evidence we found most informative for these conclusions,” METR wrote.

Anthropic’s stated aim was to remove confidential information that could help other AI developers, according to METR. The group said it took no payment, though Anthropic supplied free tokens.

On 9 November, Anthropic released Claude Fable 5.2 alongside Mythos 5.2. Its system card said Mythos 5.2 does not double the internal rate of AI research progress.

The second amendment, a day later, made the call. Anthropic based it on its expectations “rather than on what we have observed from current models.” The policy covers progress the company can “observe or expect,” the filing noted. Anthropic said it continues to assess the underlying data and will publish further reports.

The same filing said Anthropic does not expect to safely let Claude fully build its successors. That would need “meaningful human supervision,” it said. It is weighing policy updates that would require human review of certain research decisions.

The seven co-founders addressed the pace in their letter to investors, which Anthropic posted on its IPO website for the roadshow. Building AI safely may require Anthropic, and ideally the rest of the industry, to modulate that pace, they wrote, “especially when this is driven by AI systems building themselves.”

“There may be times when we choose to advance more slowly than we could in order to make our systems safer, even if that puts us at a competitive disadvantage,” the co-founders wrote.

The roadshow video pitched the speed. In its section on compute, presented by co-founder Tom Brown, Anthropic said Claude Mythos got Claude running on AMD chips in three days “with basically no human intervention,” according to Simon Willison’s transcript.

“We believe that this can accelerate even further as we approach recursive self-improvement,” the video says.

The AI Futures Project, the group behind the AI 2027 scenario, responded on 10 November. Citing METR’s report, all its authors now expect a “Superhuman AI Researcher” at some point in 2027, unless the industry deliberately slows down. They define it as an AI that matches the research taste of the best human researchers and fully automates coding.

“We strongly urge Anthropic to release enough information such that external observers can make a complete assessment,” the authors wrote.

They called for an industry-wide pause on AI research and development. Human review of the kind Anthropic described could “provide too much comfort,” they added.

Anthropic’s IPO seeks a $1.4 trillion valuation, with trading on Nasdaq due to start on 19 November.

Collaboration and teamwork are paramount to her, as she loves empowering her colleagues to achieve outstanding results and unlock their full potential.

David Leitch Wants His New Heist Thriller to Get People Talking About America's Healthcare and Debt Crisis

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From the man who brought us Deadpool 2 and The Fall Guy, director David Leitch’s new crime thriller, How to Rob a Bank, is set to release in theaters soon. But before you catch the getaway car, don’t miss our interview with Leitch, where he discusses why this star-filled feature is so much more than an action-packed heist movie.

In How to Rob a Bank, Nicholas Hoult is The Wolf, an anonymous YouTuber whose merry band of bank robbers — Pete Davidson, Anna Sawai, and Rhenzy Feliz — go viral broadcasting their heists on their channel. As they continue to garner more views, an FBI agent (John C. Reilly) enlists the help of a hacker under house arrest (Zoë Kravitz) to help track them down.

During this interview, Leitch talks with Collider’s Steve Weintraub about the movie’s social commentary and the techniques he used to give the movie a more grounded approach. He shares his hopes for audiences after seeing the movie, the kinds of conversations that inspired him to make How to Rob a Bank, the crucial element he added that wasn’t in the original script, plus teases his next movie, Jason Statham Stole My Bike. Check out the full conversation in the video above, with a transcript provided below.

COLLIDER: I'm doing a supercut right now of everyone I'm talking to, and the question is, where do you like to sit in a movie theater?

DAVID LEITCH: Me personally, I like to sit halfway up on an aisle.

LEITCH: It's always an aisle, and it's really just because if I do feel like I have to go to the bathroom, I just want to not disturb anybody. But otherwise, the reason halfway up is I want to see the whole screen. I don't want to get too close where it's not in my peripheral. I like to see the actual edges of the screen. But it's just more of my compositional mind, and as I'm watching it, I'm also watching it as a filmmaker.

What do you want to tell people about the best title of 2027: your next film, which is Jason Statham Stole My Bike?

LEITCH: I want to tell them that it's as fun as the title itself. That's all you need to say. It's as fun as the title itself. The title says it all. It's a blast.

LEITCH: I have a really good idea, but I can't say yet.

LEITCH: You won’t pressure me? That's your job! No, I do, but I can't.

One of the things I think is really cool about How to Rob a Bank is that you shot a lot of dramatic sequences on 35 millimeter. Talk a little bit about filming the movie on 35 millimeter.

LEITCH: There were two formats going on. You had this crazy content creator story that we were getting to use all the new technology — the Osmo cameras, the Insta360 — to create these fun, subversive edutainment episodes of “The Wolf.” I actually had a blast.

I really wanted to ground the real-life drama, the snapshot of the world we're in right now, and to do that, I chose 35. I think it gives it a completely different look compositionally. I chose a very different style. And juxtaposed, those two worlds got to intertwine somewhere in the middle of the movie.

It was just really a choice, to be honest. I hadn't shot film in a while, and coming back to it was great. There is something that makes you feel like you're capturing reality when you shoot film, even though digital is essentially more real in a sense.

I think there's a different energy on set when you're shooting on 35.

LEITCH: Yeah, yeah, yeah. For sure. Everyone's more on top of it. Everyone's keyed in. You're cutting between takes. You create this sort of atmosphere to deliver on the performance at the given moment. It's just a fact.

When you were making this film, there was a lot of anger about what's going on in America with the haves and have-nots, but now with the movie coming out, I think that the timing couldn't be better. Can you talk about what the film is leaning into and how it relates to what's going on right now?

LEITCH: That's one of the main reasons I took on the project. I think what the material was talking about felt so now. It was very, very contemporary. I think these characters, although you don't really find out their motivations until the second act, which I've held off that reveal by choice, they're very relatable. People are dealing with health care debt, people are dealing with homes they can't afford, people are dealing with medical issues, and the system is not necessarily working for them.

Then, even though it's a traditional heist movie where it's a cat-and-mouse, you also have an FBI agent who's struggling with his identity. He’s like, “I'm chasing these people, but I understand their problems.” He’s like, “I just want to protect people.” He's altruistic, and the system's not working for him either.

I'd hoped that people could leave the film and have conversations surrounding this. That's what's really important to me. I think we don't talk enough about these issues. I don't think people speak up enough about them. If you can leave this theater and you feel inspired to change something, then that's great. Find a way to change it. You want to go out and vote? You want to go out and have a peaceful protest? You should do it. If you want to have conversations with your friends and change them on their worldview, that's great.

That's what art is supposed to do, and that's what this film is supposed to do, outside of it being a big, fun commercial heist movie. I think I set that up in the first act, but then I'm taking you on a journey.

Leitch also gives a major update on 'Jason Statham Stole My Bike' and 'Gears of War.'

You know I love talking about editing because it's where it all comes together. So you show the movie to people early. What did you learn from those early screenings that impacted the finished film?

LEITCH: I think we had two early screenings, and you learn things about what's working with the relatability of the characters and what's not. You learn tonally upfront I'm playing with humor at the beginning of the movie, like the subversive show that the Wolf is creating, How to Rob a Bank. Are they engaged enough? Are they laughing at those moments? How do I make those episodes more fun and more irreverent?

So I took away things there, and I took away things about how to just refine these characters so that emotional payoff really lands in the third act, and I don't want to give that away. But there are, again, things you learn in testing where people are tracking the beats you want them to do, and you make those slight adjustments so the audience can go on a ride.

A movie like this is not conventional. I think usually you know the motivations of the robbers up front, right? So you can easily go on this journey with them. Here, I hold it off, hold it off, hold it off, and I turn it on its head towards the second half of the movie. You're in a totally different film at that point, and I think in a great way. But to land that turn took some finesse, for sure.

I really enjoy the YouTube things where Nick [Hoult] is talking to the camera and educating people on how fucked things are, pardon my language. Talk a little bit about putting those sequences together, because those are very important to the movie. Those have to work, or the whole thing doesn't work.

LEITCH: So a lot of that material wasn't in the original script. It was really about a bunch of content creators that were posting a how-to guide to rob a bank, and it was more technical. It was less about joining the resistance and more about, like, these are the technical things you need to do to rob a bank. I got more excited about what would their ideologies be, right? And then how do you, again, educate a population?

So, I created a bunch of episodes inside the scripts that attacked specific things that I think are relatable to people today, things that they're struggling with, commentary that seems relevant for our time, as relevant as the characters that were already on the page — dealing with health care debt, dealing with loss, dealing with medical things they can't control, and the cost of living that's out of control, and this massive transfer of wealth from a generation that is not ever going to see it.

The YouTube show became sort of my big experiment of, like, again, drafting real episodes that had something to say, and that hopefully the audience can talk about later, like if it's going to move the needle on how they feel they want to move forward in their own lives of, like, can I change the system or not, and how can I be part of that?

How to Rob a Bank opens in theaters on November 13.

'A Massive Mistake': Tech, AI and Crypto Founders React to Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Exit

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

'A Massive Mistake': Tech, AI and Crypto Founders React to Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Exit

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang is out as CEO after 33 years, and some of the loudest names in crypto and artificial intelligence think the company just made a spectacular mistake.

On Thursday, Nvidia confirmed Huang’s resignation as president, CEO, and board member, and named longtime finance chief Colette Kress interim CEO. The move followed reports of two extramarital affairs, including one involving a businesswoman U.S. intelligence reportedly suspected of links to China, as well as concerns over a reported relationship with an Nvidia employee.

Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees on Sunday. Nvidia said in a board statement that it had no reason to believe its secrets or national security were compromised, and its independent investigation remains underway. The allegations about foreign intelligence have not established that Huang knowingly provided information to a foreign government.

But even without any leaks, the scandal was enough for the board to decide to let him go.

The market did not welcome the transition. Nvidia shares fell 14% at Thursday’s opening bell; with more than $500 billion wiped from U.S. stocks in a single minute. The company that was once the most valuable in the world has now fallen to third on that list.

Nvidia was already under pressure before the scandal, and Huang’s departure raised a particular problem for a company built so closely around its founder: Who, exactly, can replace him?

Changpeng “CZ” Zhao, the Binance co-founder and one of crypto’s most influential executives, said “it's a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign.” Bitcoin entrepreneur and former Kraken executive Dan Held agreed, writing, “He shouldn't have resigned,” and argued that Huang’s personal problems should be weighed against the company he built.

It's a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign.

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 12, 2026

Erik Voorhees, who founded ShapeShift and privacy-focused AI platform Venice, was more confrontational. “The entire coup against Jensen Huang can be halted by asking a single question,” he wrote. “What evidence is there that Alice Huang is actually a Chinese spy? The answer, unsurprisingly, is absolutely zero.”

U.S. intelligence officials reportedly assessed Alice Huang as a Chinese intelligence asset, but Nvidia says it has no reason to believe any secrets were compromised, and Singaporean authorities said they lacked sufficient evidence to establish that she committed a crime. As of today, no law enforcement agency has presented any hard evidence against Alice Huang.

Anthony Pompliano, the Bitcoin investor and commentator, moved the conversation from the boardroom to the technology itself. “Do not get distracted by the headlines,” he wrote. “Open-source AI is essential to the freedom of billions of people in the future.”

Open-source AI is essential to the freedom of billions of people in the future.

— Anthony Pompliano 🌪 (@APompliano) November 12, 2026

Surrounding it all is likely the most talked about conspiracy theory in the open-source AI sphere. The ousting was designed to hurt open-source developments, the speculation goes, since Nvidia is one of the leading voices in the open-source movement, which closed-source companies see as a threat.

For Voorhees, the controversy also prompted a practical question. “Is there a decentralized Hugging Face yet?” he asked, alluding to one of Huang’s stated commitments. In other words, the crypto solution to a potential platform bottleneck is to stop trusting a single platform.

That concern is more than a slogan for a founder whose Venice AI business is built around private access to open models. Voorhees previously told Decrypt that the antidote to excessive control over AI was “open-source decentralization.”

Some developers saw a deeper threat in Huang’s departure: the loss of a powerful corporate champion of downloadable AI models, whose underlying parameters, or weights, can be run outside the companies that built them.

The worry is that an Nvidia without Huang could become less willing to confront firms that keep their most advanced models behind proprietary services.

Jun Song, an open-source AI advocate and hardware entrepreneur, alleged that OpenAI and Anthropic had spent millions lobbying Congress to restrict open weights. He pointed to a “Pace the Frontier” campaign and argued that Huang responded by building the Open Secure AI Alliance, ending his post with, “And today, we lost him.”

OpenAI and Anthropic have spent millions lobbying the US Congress to ban open weights.

When they formed the "Pace the Frontier" group to push for strict limits on open models, Jensen stepped up right away.

He created the Open Secure Alliance to fight back.

Look at the list of… pic.twitter.com/OeasotcLO3

In July, Nvidia joined Microsoft, IBM, Hugging Face, and dozens of other organizations to launch the Open Secure AI Alliance, which works on open AI security tools and standards. Nvidia also backed a letter defending open weights against broad restrictions.

The broader conspiracy claim needs, of course, much more evidence. The Pacing the Frontier initiative is a statement from employees at several AI companies seeking tools to slow dangerous frontier development, not proof of a joint corporate campaign to ban all open-weight models or remove Huang.

OpenAI subsequently signed the pro-open-weight letter, while Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said explicitly that his company had never advocated such a ban.

Other posts on social media showed how much anxiety the debate had generated. A commenter replied to Song that they were now “counting on Huawei and Deepseek for the very survival of the OSS community.”

Huawei and Deepseek are two major Chinese companies, known for their open-source developments

I can't believe now I am counting on Huawei and Deepseek for the very survival of the OSS community. What a loss...

— Look4Newways (@sflk2026) November 12, 2026

The issue comes with a rather large business backdrop. Nvidia agreed in September to acquire the open-source AI repository Hugging Face for roughly $12.9 billion, subject to regulatory clearance, in a deal expected to close in the first half of 2027.

That arrangement could put both the hardware used to build AI and the largest hub used to distribute open models under one company’s influence—arguably, the most powerful company on earth.

Epic Games founder and CEO Tim Sweeney was among the highest-profile tech executives to condemn the exit. “A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity,” he wrote, praising Huang for keeping innovation moving even as Nvidia grew into an enormous company.

A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity. Jensen founded and ran one of the few companies to reach a massive scale while driving massive continued innovation and ubiquity, while others fell into the trap of ever-increasing extraction. https://t.co/AkS5XT9HF8

— Tim Sweeney (@TimSweeneyEpic) November 12, 2026

Perplexity AI co-founder and CEO Aravind Srinivas took a more personal approach. “Thank you Jensen Huang for 33 years of bold leadership,” he wrote. He also credited Huang’s generosity toward other founders, including himself, for helping shape the future of American AI.

Peter Steinberger, creator of the open-source OpenClaw agent and now at OpenAI, argued that the decision felt rushed. “If that spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal,” he wrote on X. “Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.”

If that Spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal. Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.

— Peter Steinberger (@steipete) November 12, 2026

Financial commentator The Kobeissi Letter had urged the board to consult shareholders before any decision, arguing that there was no obvious replacement and citing research on the costs of ousting successful CEOs. After the announcement, the account called the resignation “the most costly mistake of the AI Revolution.”

Former DWS executive and corporate-governance critic Desiree Fixler invoked directors’ fiduciary duty, saying the board should protect shareholders instead of indulging what she called a media frenzy.

In general, most of the reactions were supportive, separating personal affairs from business. The corporate espionage angle has apparently not swayed tech enthusiasts.

Bitcoin and privacy advocate Neil Woodfine described Huang as a founder who had built Nvidia to the pinnacle and championed free, local AI, only to be displaced by a managerial establishment convinced it could do better.

This Jensen Huang thing is so ugly. Guy takes a company from zero to absolute pinnacle, through blood, sweat, and tears, a pure founder’s founder, great products, makes so many people rich beyond their imaginations, champions open free local AI, and then the incumbent modern…

— Neil Woodfine (@nwoodfine) November 12, 2026

Not everyone outside that chorus thought the security questions could be set aside. North Dakota Republican Sen. Kevin Cramer had called for Huang’s exit, warning about the risk of blackmail and saying the internal investigation was insufficient. The concern from Washington has been largely uniform: the possibility of compromising pressure against the leader of a company central to U.S. AI infrastructure.

Meanwhile, President Donald Trump has remained supportive of Huang: “He’s definitely suffering,” he told the press. “I think he’ll be in good shape.”

Speculation about Huang’s next job spread just as quickly as criticism of his last one. Responding to an X user asking him to hire Huang, Elon Musk replied, “That would be an honor.”

Nvidia says its board is searching for a permanent CEO from inside and outside the company, with Kress running the business in the meantime. Its next scheduled earnings report is November 17, the first major financial update investors will receive after Huang’s departure.
