Jensen Huang’s downfall cut the P/E of Nvidia stock by a third. But don’t call it a bargain yet

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The steep fall in Nvidia’s stock that started with revelations about CEO Jensen Huang’s romantic relationship with an alleged Chinese spy, and accelerated following his resignation on November 12, makes Nvidia’s shares look like a historic buy. At least that’s the conclusion from analyzing its numbers by conventional metrics. The GPU-maker’s shares peaked on October 5 at $243. At that point, Nvidia sported the world’s biggest market cap at $5.85 trillion. Wall Street analysts following the stock were almost universal even then in acclaiming Nvidia as a cheap stock. And, it appeared, for good reason: Nvidia was selling at 30 times its earnings over the previous four quarters, a modest number for an enterprise growing its bottom line at an annual clip of 100%.

Today, Nvidia looks like a far greater bargain based strictly on the numbers. Since the summit in early October, it’s tumbled 34% as of mid-morning on November 12 to $161, a downdraft that slashed nearly $2 trillion from its valuation, the biggest dollar drop by far, in a condensed period, in the history of capital markets. As a result, Nvidia’s PE has dropped to 20, a figure that given its current stats, tags it as practically the ultimate value play. For example, Apple and Amazon carry a PE of 40 and 22 respectively, and are expanding at a fraction of Nvidia’s speed.

Indeed, despite its immense size and sorcerous growth rate, Nvidia has so far shown no signs of falling victim to the law of large numbers. In the quarter ended October 31, its sales jumped 26% over the previous three-month span, beating the around 20% jumps of the two previous quarters. What about profitability? In the October quarter, gross margins hit a sumptuous 75%, beating its FY 2026 posting of 71.3%.

Make a sober assessment of Nvidia’s overall condition, however, and the best conclusion is that the market is probably right in imposing this giant discount based on Huang’s departure. Nvidia’s wild profitability arises from its virtual monopoly in the top GPU segment. According to a top industry executive who’s an Nvidia customer, it is 18 to 24 months ahead of all-comers in the quality and performance of its top chips. The rub, says this person, is Nvidia’s dependence on Huang as both an unmatched innovator and excellent operator. “Huang was the tech visionary and he also got Nvidia to operate incredibly well,” says this person. “The question is how much depended on Jensen, and how much of what he did can another person do?”

This executive stresses that Nvidia has maintained its huge margins by keeping several paces ahead of the field, and never slowing, one of the greatest feats in the annals of tech. “That continuing advantage in chips is absolutely essential to Nvidia. It’s an industry where you can’t miss a step. If you do, others will catch up fast. Intel fell behind, and it never caught up.”

Plus, competitors who regularly complain about the high cost of Nvidia chips are racing to create their own, in-house competitors, a list that encompasses AMD, Google, and Amazon.

It’s also revealing to parse how Nvidia’s deploying its huge free cash flow to stay in front. In the past four quarters, it’s generated $105 billion in FCF. In the past, it’s channeled a huge portion of that bounty into share buybacks. But in the 12 months ended in October, repurchases fell to just $32 billion. Nvidia’s cash Hoard of $100 billion in relatively modest, and growing slowly.

So where is all the rest of that capex going? Nvidia is famously making big investments in its customers, including SpaceX, CoreWeave, OpenAI, SoftBank’s SB Energy, and Intel (for CPUs). It’s also provided multibillion-dollar credit guarantees for projects built for hyperscalers and neoclouds where it’s providing the GPUs. The practice of funding its own customers is often questioned as “circular financing.” The question is whether Huang felt obliged to follow that course to protect Nvidia’s future. The reason: Locking in future sales as rivals hatch competing products at lower price points. In other words, Huang sought to forestall the commoditization of Nvidia, the threat that’s eventually hit the profitability of all tech hardware giants.

And that’s just the problem. Huge prices and margins inevitably attract cheaper new supply. The market judges that Nvidia’s margins and profit growth will look far lower in the future than in the past, and that losing the genius who could hold commoditization at bay longest was a big, big loss.

A former CEO whom I spoke to took a contrarian stance by arguing that the Nvidia board made a big mistake in not making every attempt to keep Huang. “I would have liked to see more facts, and maybe the board’s got them,” says this person. “The board was not thinking about shareholders. They were thinking about their reputations and not getting sued. They should have punished Huang and made it a very visible punishment like no equity or bonus for a couple of years. They should have found a different way to punish the guy that didn’t damage shareholders.”

This person admits that this is probably a minority view. “People love to get on their high moral horse and that’s what we’re seeing,” says the observer. “You can’t just plug and play to replace someone so critical to the creation of a company’s one-of-a-kind products and operation. A number of CEOs who couldn’t say it publicly would probably say that the Nvidia board forgot what the role of a board should be, to protect shareholders, not themselves.”

That breathtaking, $2 trillion loss in value may be an enduring number, and not just the result of panic selling that most on Wall Street claim.

Shawn Tully is a senior editor-at-large at Fortune, covering the biggest trends in business, aviation, politics, and leadership.

Xbox CEO’s 25th Anniversary Message Is About AI, Even If She Won’t Say It

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As Xbox celebrates its 25th anniversary, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma has shared her thoughts on where gaming is going over the next 25 years, and people are saying her message is really more about pushing AI.

Sharma said modern games demonstrate the "heights of human craft in ways unimaginable just decades ago." However, the costs, resources, and risks that companies need to take on have "grown just as dramatically," she said.

"As a result, too many ideas never become games, and too many games fall short of what their creators imagined. For decades, this has been the unavoidable tradeoff of ambition. We must change this," she said.

Historically, to create anything at scale required immense resources, but Sharma said that's changing. Without saying AI, Sharma said it won't be long until a single person can "wield capabilities that once required entire nations."

"A small team will be able to build worlds that today demand hundreds of developers and hundreds of millions of dollars. And our largest studios will be able to pursue ambitions as far beyond today’s games as Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024 is from its first entry in 1982," she said.

Sharma went on to say that the future of gaming "will look more like its beginning," which is to say that a small team with a bold idea can make a real impact. "Our responsibility is to deliver great games, unmistakable hardware, and give more people the chance to make something the world has never seen," she said.

Xbox recently launched Xbox Labs, which is a pretty obvious push into the world of vibe-coded games. Sharma has promised that she has "no tolerance for bad AI." She added, “We will not chase short-term efficiency or flood our ecosystem with soulless AI slop.”

Sharma became Xbox CEO earlier this year, replacing Phil Spencer. Sharma immediately made lots of big changes to "reset" the business, including mass layoffs, game cancellations, and studio closures/divestitures.

Sharma comes from an AI background, having previously been an executive on the CoreAI team at Microsoft. She may be bullish about having smaller teams make big games going forward, but it's not an all-or-nothing approach. After all, Xbox continues to own and fund large-scale, expensive productions like Call of Duty, World of Warcraft, and Forza just to name a few.

AI is a big push for Xbox's owner, Microsoft, so it makes sense to see Sharma undertake efforts to increase Xbox's own efforts in that department. This is happening after a study of game developers found that more developers than ever think generative AI is bad for gaming.

Constellation Space launches its platform for managing fleets of satellites with AI tools

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chronicling the Seattle and Pacific Northwest startup scene. SEE MORE

by Alan Boyle on Nov 12, 2026 at 9:10 amNovember 12, 2026 at 9:11 am

Seattle-based Constellation Space is rolling out its flagship software platform, which uses machine learning to streamline operations for fleets of satellites and the ground networks that serve them.

“We build models in-house for things like weather, attenuation, signal jamming, demand … and synthesize all of that to help operators understand what their ground contact plan looks like in the future, and how to optimize their fleets,” CEO Kamran Majid told GeekWire.

The Constellation Platform is tailor-made for a growing number of satellite constellations in low Earth orbit. Keeping track of thousands of satellites — and the best opportunities for downlinking data from each of those satellites — is becoming an increasingly complicated task.

Constellation Space, which was founded last year, draws upon Majid’s experience as a NASA engineering intern and as a software engineer working on SpaceX’s Starlink satellite network.

“At Starlink, I saw firsthand how manual mission-control processes break down as constellations scale,” he said. “I started Constellation to help smaller teams get ahead of link failures instead of spending their time rebuilding pass plans and recovering from outages.”

Majid’s fellow co-founders include chief technology officer Raaid Kabir, a veteran of Jeff Bezos’ Blue Origin space venture, and Omeed Tehrani and Laith Altarabishi, who were previously software engineers at Capital One. “We’re a team of six people right now,” Majid said.

One year into its existence, Constellation Space has already gained traction. Several months ago, the startup said it raised $7.5 million in a seed round backed by Y Combinator, Nvidia, OpenAI, Samsung Next and other investors.

Majid said an early version of the Constellation Platform has attracted more than 10 customers, including Amazon Web Services. It’s also used by more than 20 academic institutions. “Our annual contract value this year is $2.25 million,” he said.

The platform combines forecasts for factors affecting satellite transmissions — including space situational awareness, weather conditions, signal-to-noise ratios, signal jamming and bandwidth demand — to predict how many gigabytes of data can be transmitted during a given pass. Operators can then use that data to optimize transmission settings and schedules.

Its machine-learning model is pre-trained on data from a global network of sensors. Once it’s deployed, the model adapts to each fleet’s specific hardware and ground stations. Constellation Space says satellite operators can boost downlinked data per pass by as much as 36%.

Majid said the journey from SpaceX to startup has been “really interesting.”

“The mindset is very similar,” he said. “I was previously an engineer. Now I’m finding myself in more of a business role. The mindset is just ‘first-principles thinking’ about everything. Eliminate all the requirements that don’t make sense before you even start to think about solving the problem. Those core tenets have stayed as we’ve progressed as a venture, and I think that’s been really important to our success so far.”

Setting up the startup in the Seattle area, where more than half of the world’s satellites are built, has also been interesting.

“We are a Y Combinator company, so we were in San Francisco for a couple of months, and there was a lot of pressure from the community in SF to have everyone be in San Francisco. We definitely felt that a little bit,” Majid said.

“But operating in the satellite sector of the space industry, Seattle is becoming more and more of a hub for the space industry in general,” he said. “Space companies are either completely moving here, opening up headquarters here, or just branching out here. It’s that, mixed in with Seattle just being known as a tech hub — and also, we all love Seattle.”

Jensen Huang resigns: A timeline of the Nvidia affair

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The AI boom has put chatbots at work, AI-generated videos in our feeds, and data centers on the agenda at town halls. Nvidia, a company that got its start making computer games look better, now supplies the chips that power much of the AI industry. That made Nvidia one of the world's most valuable companies and its chips some of the most sought-after technology in the world.

And on Thursday, Nov. 12, the company announced that it had lost the only CEO it had ever had.

The story goes back more than a year and reads a little like an HBO drama, with alleged spies, extramarital affairs, and corporate intrigue. Let's break down the timeline of the Jensen Huang resignation based on what came to light and when.

Last year, the New York Times reported that U.S. officials were looking into whether Nvidia chips meant for data centers in Southeast Asia had ended up in China.

The chips in question had been bought by Megaspeed, a Singapore company that rented out computing power. U.S. export rules limit China's access to some of Nvidia’s most advanced AI hardware, to protect Nvidia's trade secrets and the United States' competitive edge over China in the AI arms race. Singapore police were also looking into whether Megaspeed had broken local laws, but at the time, Nvidia said it had no reason to believe the chips had been sent elsewhere.

The Times' report referred to Megaspeed's "mystery C.E.O." and noted U.S. officials were looking into the company's ties to China.

Things were quiet for a while; until two weeks ago, when the Times reported that U.S. intelligence officials suspected Alice Huang, a former Megaspeed executive, of gathering information for Beijing. Officials had learned that Alice Huang had developed a close personal relationship with Jensen Huang. (Yes, the two share a surname. No, they are not related.)

Nvidia initially characterized their interactions as routine meetings with a customer: "Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected," an Nvidia spokesperson told TechCrunch in an email on Oct. 29, adding that "meetings with customers are part of a C.E.O.’s job."

The Times also reported that intelligence officials had briefed President Donald Trump about the relationship roughly a year earlier. By then, Jensen Huang had already spent months pressing the Trump administration to let Nvidia sell more advanced AI chips to China.

Flash forward to Nov. 5, when the chairs of three House committees publicly asked Jensen Huang for a closed-door briefing. They wanted to know whether his ties to Alice Huang could have given Beijing access to sensitive U.S. technology or information, and what Nvidia was doing to guard against foreign espionage.

The next day, the New York Times reported that Secretary of State Marco Rubio had overseen the drafting of a classified memo this spring. It described U.S. intelligence suggesting that China's Ministry of State Security had recruited Alice Huang to target the Nvidia CEO. As the chairs of the House Foreign Affairs wrote, "Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information." Meanwhile, the Financial Times reported that U.S. intelligence officials believed the relationship was romantic. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Want more tech and digital culture news delivered to your inbox daily or sent straight to your device? Sign up for Mashable's Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

On Sunday, Nov. 8, Nvidia made the rumors official. The company published an email Huang sent to employees. "I was unfaithful to my wife," he wrote. "There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry."

He did not name Alice Huang, though the New York Times reported that he'd privately told Nvidia’s board he had been intimate with her. Huang said he would cooperate with the investigation. A few hours later, Nvidia's board announced the investigation in a separate statement and said its independent directors would oversee it with outside counsel. The board said it had no reason at that point to believe national security or company secrets had been compromised and expressed confidence in Huang and his leadership team.

On Monday, Trump told reporters he'd asked Jensen Huang whether he knew about the spying allegations involving Alice Huang. "He said 'I had no idea that she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump said. China's foreign ministry spokesperson, Guo Jiakun, dismissed the accusation. "The 'Chinese spy' allegation is purely unsubstantiated and directed at smearing China," he exclaimed.

That same day, Nvidia shareholder Mark Kistenmacher filed a proposed class action alleging that the company and its CEO had misled investors about Huang's ties to the former Megaspeed executive.

The next day, Nvidia's independent directors said they'd hired Joon H. Kim, a former acting U.S. attorney in Manhattan, and his firm, Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, to lead their investigation.

The story continued to unravel Wednesday, as the New York Times reported that Huang also had an affair with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare and one of his direct reports (of which he has more than 60). The Wall Street Journal later reported that Nvidia was preparing to remove him, and Reuters reported that the board met that evening to discuss his future.

On Thursday morning, Nvidia officially announced Huang's departure after 33 years as CEO and named longtime CFO Colette Kress interim CEO. An SEC filing released that morning said the board had accepted Huang's resignation as CEO, president, and director the previous evening, effective immediately. Kress will keep her finance role while the board considers internal and external candidates for the permanent job. Nvidia also named longtime director Harvey C. Jones its first board chair.

Also on Thursday, Singapore's home affairs ministry said Alice Huang had left the country in February and had been barred from re-entering following a local investigation involving possible offenses tied to server purchases and exports. The ministry said investigators found insufficient evidence to establish that she had committed an offense, and that its inquiry did not concern espionage. Megaspeed, for its part, said it had cooperated with U.S. inspections and hired a law firm to independently review its export compliance.

Nvidia is scheduled to release its quarterly results around 4:20 p.m. ET on Tuesday, Nov. 17, followed by an earnings call at 5 p.m. ET. It will be the company’s first earnings call since Jensen Huang resigned and Kress took over as interim CEO.

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Prima di Italia-Francia, il ct lega il momento del centrocampista alla continuità trovata nella squadra di Gattuso.

Roberto Mancini elogia Davide Frattesi alla vigilia di Italia-Francia, sfida di Nations League in programma allo stadio Giuseppe Meazza di Milano. Il commissario tecnico azzurro indica il centrocampista della Lazio tra i giocatori dal rendimento migliore in questo momento. Ai microfoni della Rai parla anche della partita e delle esclusioni di Liberali e Cisse.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Nel presentare la sfida, Mancini si sofferma sul periodo del centrocampista biancoceleste. Frattesi sta trovando spazio con continuità nella Lazio allenata da Gennaro Gattuso e ha assunto un ruolo importante negli equilibri della squadra. Il ct collega proprio alla regolarità d’impiego i progressi dell’ex giocatore dell’Inter.

«Frattesi sta giocando con continuità alla Lazio, quindi ha trovato probabilmente la sua identità. Sta bene, gioca, è tranquillo. In questo momento sicuramente è uno dei migliori per rendimento». Le parole di Mancini accompagnano l’avvicinamento del centrocampista all’impegno con la Nazionale, dopo le prestazioni offerte in maglia biancoceleste.

A poche ore dall’incontro con la Francia, Mancini richiama l’attenzione sulla prova che chiede agli Azzurri. Il ct vuole che la squadra giochi bene e ottenga un buon risultato, anche per non deludere i tifosi. Sottolinea inoltre la risposta del pubblico quando la Nazionale scende in campo a San Siro.

«Dovremo fare una bella partita perché altrimenti i tifosi ci rimarranno male. Dovremo cercare di giocare bene e fare un buon risultato. E comunque San Siro fa sempre il pieno quando arriva la Nazionale». È questa l’aspettativa espressa dal selezionatore per la sfida con i francesi, nella quale Frattesi punta a confermare in azzurro quanto mostrato con la Lazio.

Mancini chiarisce anche la scelta di lasciare fuori Liberali e Cisse dall’elenco dei calciatori disponibili per la partita. I due giovani sono tra gli ultimi arrivati nel gruppo e il commissario tecnico richiama sia l’impegno previsto tra tre giorni sia il numero dei giocatori presenti.

«È una scelta perché comunque sono gli ultimi arrivati e abbiamo una partita tra tre giorni. Quindi oggi è stata questa la scelta. Siamo in 28 e purtroppo qualcuno deve andare in tribuna». L’esclusione dei due calciatori rientra così, nelle parole di Mancini, nella gestione del gruppo in vista degli impegni ravvicinati.

These Quick &amp; Easy Friendsgiving Recipes Are Perfect For Last-Minute Party Invites

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you or your friends are procrastinators, save these recipes.

Some people love to plan Friendsgiving weeks in advance with spreadsheets and personalized table settings, and others, well, let’s just say panicked group texts a few hours before dinner aren’t uncommon. While we’ll never be mad at a Friendsgiving invitation, late-notice parties don’t allow a lot of time to prepare time-intensive casseroles, apps, and sides. That’s why we’ve rounded up our favorite quick and easy holiday recipes that are perfect for when you’ve gotta run to a party, but still want to show up with something fun, festive, and delicious. These recipes either come together in under an hour (some under 30 minutes!) or use store-bought hacks that make preparing them a breeze. So if you get a party invitation for the next day, no need to show up with just a bottle of wine—these recipes will make you seem more prepared than the host (and you may just be!).

We engineered this bite-sized app to have everything you could ever want in a holiday appetizer, all in one bite: a flaky pastry base made from crescent dough, sweet cranberry sauce, rich, cheesy brie, and crunchy pecans. Ready in a little over 30 minutes, these bites are the ideal last-minute appetizer.

In our opinion, Brussels sprouts are the unsung heroes of the holiday season. Their earthy, nutty, slightly sweet flavor is perfect for comforting dishes, and, even if you aren't traditionally a fan of Brussels sprouts, this cheesy casserole is here to prove it.

Pumpkin pie is a Friendsgiving must-have, but what to do when there are more diners than slices? Or, what if you're more of a crust-lover than filling? Enter these adorable mini pumpkin pies, which rethink the crust-to-filling ratio in the most adorable, snackable package.

This herby, savory-sweet dish is basically Thanksgiving in meatball form. With a sweet and tangy glaze made with cranberry sauce and thyme- and sage-speckled turkey meatballs, all you need is a scoop of fluffy mashed potatoes for cozy, autumnal bliss.

Think glazed sweet potatoes always need brown sugar, orange juice, or pecans? Think again. Coated in a savory maple-miso sauce, these melting sweet potatoes are here to challenge everything you think a sweet potato can be—and win you over with their deliciousness.

When we want to incorporate this veggie into our Thanksgiving dinner, we turn to this fresh, seasonal salad. With toasted almonds, shaved Parmesan, and pomegranate seeds, this is the holiday side that will convert even the biggest Brussels sprout haters.

Few dishes generate as many internet debates and passionate opinions as a classic mac and cheese. We all know it. We all love it. And we all think we know the very best way to make it. But, this four-ingredient, 20-minute Boursin mac is one that everyone can get behind.

Sweet and savory with a kick of heat, these pumpkin, Brie, and hot honey bites are the perfect fall appetizer to serve during the holidays, or really at any dinner party. The honey provides the perfect balance for the sweetness of the pumpkin and the savory, melty Brie.

A holiday cheeseboard is great and all, but why go to all the trouble when you can make this sweet, salty, creamy, and tart dip with a fraction of the effort Fresh feta cheese is combined with herbs, garlic, and cream cheese, whipped until super-smooth, then topped with tart homemade cranberry sauce, toasted pistachios, and honey.

Here, we stuff our crescent rolls—shaped in a shareable, stunning ring—with all our favorites: mashed potatoes, stuffing, turkey, and cranberry sauce. Served with gravy for dunking (of course), this Thanksgiving ring is the simple, easy appetizer.

Take-Two CEO Says Discs Will Become Gaming’s Vinyl: ‘It’s Going To Be A Very Small Part Of The Business’

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

DiscsGTA 6Physical GamesPlayStationRockstar GamesVinyl

Grand Theft Auto 6 is the first major console game to release without a physical version. Take-Two CEO Strauss Zelnick doesn’t think it’ll be the last. Physical games will hang around, but not in any mass-market sort of way. He says they’ll become closer to the equivalent of vinyl records in music.

âIt may not be the end, but listen, weâve been talking, you and I, for a long time,â he told The Game Business‘ Christopher Dring in a new interview. âI predicted that digital distribution would be well over 90 percent of the business more than 10 years ago. I donât always get it right, but this one I got right. Will there always be some physical business? Yes, in the same way that thereâs still a vinyl business in recorded music. But itâs going to be a very small part of the business.â

Physical music accounted for just over 10 percent of total music revenue in the first half of 2026, according to estimates by theÂ Recording Industry Association of America (RIAA). That comes out to $581 million, roughly 80 percent of which was earned through sales of vinyl.

According to Circana, physical game sales in the U.S. last year hit a 30-year low of $1.5 billion. That was about 2.5 percent of the $60.7 billion consumers spent on âvideo game hardware, content, and accessoriesâ in 2025. Physical game sales are already a smaller chunk of overall game spending than vinyl is for music, and that’s before PlayStation game discs go away entirely in 2028. Game discs becoming vinyl would actually be a rosier outlook than where things currently seem to be trending.

We still don’t know how Microsoft will approach physical media with the launch of its Project Helix family of next-gen consoles, and Nintendo has confirmed it has no plans to abandon physical games during the Switch 2 era, even if it’s already taken a half-step in that direction with game key cards.

Will GTA 6 ever get a disc version? Vinyl has been a way for music lovers to immortalize their favorite albums. Surely the biggest game launch ever deserves something similar? Zelnick previously shot down rumors that GTA 6 wasn’t coming to disc. He’s now demurred on whether it could get a physical release in the future. That sort of âinformation always comes from our labels,” he told Variety this week, leaving the ball in Rockstar’s court to decide.

GTA 6 is getting a vinyl release for its soundtrack, however. Seem a bit hypocritical? It’s probably less surprising when, as revealed in Take-Two’s latest financials, only 2 percent of its current revenue comes from game discs.

These are the 20 best books of 2026, according to Amazon editors

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Amazon just dropped its best books of 2026 list. Earlier this year, the editors over at Amazon rounded up the best books of the year so far, but now that the year is nearly wrapped, the definitive list is here.

"We saw people reach for sweeping family sagas, genre-bending fiction, gripping narrative nonfiction, and horror," says Amazon Editor Al Woodworth. The end-of-year list is packed with books across genres, and this year, the editors at Amazon even added two new best-of lists on horror and buzzworthy books.

Picking the top 20 books of the year is a big task. Woodworth says that Amazon Editors are reading thousands of books every year, then championing the titles they can't stop thinking about. "At the end of the year, we look back at everything we've read and debate which books belong on the final list. Every reader is different, so we recommend books from the widest possible selection," says Woodworth.

There are common themes across the top books of the year, including non-fiction that reads like fiction, stories of sacrificing for those they love, characters facing hard choices, and books the internet can't stop talking about.

But the best book of the year? Well, read on to find out which novel Woodworth and fellow Amazon Editors declared the best of the year.

Earlier this year, Amazon Editor Annabel Gutterman spoke on Night Objects, saying: “This mesmerizing suspense novel about a teenager’s desperate search for belonging at her elite boarding school — where secrets swirl, and danger looms large — lived in my head for days. Eli Raphael introduces herself as a writer you don’t want to miss.”

Fans of Alison Bechdel's Fun Home will love the 2026 release The Nightingales by Emil Wilson. This graphic novel is a touching picture of unlikely friendship between a 17-year-old girl and the dying stranger who moves into her family home. It's visually and emotionally striking, a graphic novel that's full of hope.

The Disappearers came out with a bang as the New York Times declared it "the novel of the decade, if not the century." It's a propulsive crime story that manages to be both harrowing and hilarious as it captures the gay experience in 1980s Jamaica.

From Gutterman: “When a tradwife influencer wakes up to discover it’s somehow the 19th century, her journey back to reality makes for a brilliant, biting novel about motherhood, fame, and faith. A reading experience like no other, this is the book I want to talk about with literally everyone I've ever met.”

From the author of Shuggie Bain, Douglas Stuart, comes a new novel, John of John. When John-Calum 'Cal' Macleod returns to his home on the Isle of Harris, it is not the place that has changed, but him. At odds with his father's expectations, Cal discovered the secrets within his home and what's truly holding the community together.

Earlier this year, Sarah Gelman, Amazon Editorial Director, said: "Strangers is a divorce memoir, but it’s also a forensic examination of a love and a marriage gone wrong, seemingly without any warning. Burden puts words to many of our worst fears — that one day the person we love and trust the most will become an utter stranger to us.”

As a romance reader, there's one book I haven't stopped hearing about. Bad Words by Rioghnach Robinson is a will-they-won't-they romance of a novelist and the critic who panned his debut book. But don't take it from me. Emily Henry calls this novel a "once-in-a-lifetime read."

As if releasing the hit film, Teenage Sex and Death at Camp Miasma wasn't enough, director Jane Schoenbrun also released the novel Public Access Afterworld. The debut is being heralded as one of the top books of the year as it delves into the surreal broadcasts of Public Access Afterworld, a pirate TV network with the potential to change lives.

Luna, Phoenix, Queen is an intricate novel, unraveling the relationship between two college professors and their resilient dog, Luna. Julie Orringer's latest novel is a web of artistic and marital betrayal, and of all those tangled in that story.

The popularity of epic family sagas continues with Mary Beth Keane's Whale Harbor, a novel told across the globe. Set in Ireland, England, Montana, and New York, the story is based on Keane's actual family history following the haunting mystery that hangs over a family when one of its members disappears.

From Woodworth: “Two-time Pulitzer Prize winner Colson Whitehead delivers another searing page-turner of heists, gangsters, criminals, and families in this mesmerizing portrait of 1980s New York City.”

From Lindsay Powers, Amazon Editor: “This heart-pounding, reported memoir sucked me in from the first page; it’s a gripping account of resilience and perseverance that goes far beyond the headlines to reveal what really happened to Evan Gershkovich when he was snatched off the street and thrown into a Russian prison.”

From Gelman: “The many fans of Big Little Lies will love this follow-up, but you don’t need to have read that book or seen the show to enjoy Big Little Truths. I’ve been a fan of Liane Moriarty since her early books, and this is simply one of her very best. The dialogue is so clever, and the storytelling so engrossing, you won’t be able to put this book down.”

From Woodworth: “Illuminating and unforgettable, Patrick Radden Keefe’s latest delivers another knockout work of narrative nonfiction, this time telling the shocking story of how a young man got caught up in the dangerous and powerful world of Russian oligarchs and landed at the bottom of the Thames.”

Woodworth says, “Never before have a 19th-century mapmaker, a scruffy, loyal dog, and a mysterious copse of trees been more enchanting. O’Farrell delivers a soaring and bewitching tale of family, and how the remote Irish peninsula where they live defines and shapes them, generation after generation.”

From Abby Abell, Amazon Editor: "Walking the line between hysterical and horrifying, this electric psychological thriller ruthlessly takes on cultural appropriation through a delicious cat-and-mouse game like no other. If you’re in a reading slump, Fruit Fly is the cure.”

From Erin Kodicek, Amazon Editor: “This was our pick for the best book of the year so far, and our love for this novel about two motherless friends coming of age in the Jim Crow South endures. A nuanced portrait of friendship, family, and race — Jones's sentences sing.”

From Powers: “Robert Kolker’s enthralling medical mystery is both mesmerizing and heartbreaking — it asks big questions about chance and choice, what makes a full life, what we’re willing to run toward (or hide from), and how we care for each other. It’s a true story that will stick with you.”

From Gutterman: “A sparkling, genre-busting gift of a novel, Emily St. John Mandel’s latest is dystopian fiction that shook me to my core. Spanning continents, timelines, and perhaps even universes, Exit Party makes for a glorious puzzle, one with pieces that fit together seamlessly.”

From Woodworth: “An extraordinary Trojan horse of a novel that will make your heart sing and make you want to talk to everybody you know about it. Utterly magnetic, American Hagwon will change the way you think about family, sacrifice, and education. Min Jin Lee has written (another) once-in-a-lifetime read.”

8 scams the FBI says target older Americans, from romance cons to caregiver theft

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The FBI names 8 fraud schemes aimed at older Americans, from fake suitors to trusted caregivers. See how each works and how to stop it.

Criminals go after older Americans on purpose, and a single successful scheme can take a serious share of what a victim has saved. The average victim over 60 reported losing more than $38,000, according to the FBI. Someone living on retirement savings may never be able to earn back a loss that size, so knowing how to stop these schemes matters as much as knowing how they work.

Losses from cybercrime make up only one part of the money taken from older people. Victims over 60 reported losing more than $7.7 billion to cybercrime in 2025, according to the FBI. Financial exploitation of every kind, not only crime committed online, costs older adults an estimated $28.3 billion a year, according to an AARP study reported by the Federal Reserve. Complaint totals count only the victims who come forward, so some of the money taken from older people may never appear in a complaint at all. The way these crimes are sorted makes the losses even harder to count. One scam often hands its victim off to another, so a loss filed under one heading may have begun under a different one. Many of these schemes are also run by organized networks that work together, rather than by lone callers, and those networks make stolen money harder to trace and to recover.

The FBI set out to identify the typical fraud schemes that criminals use against elderly victims, from fake suitors on dating sites to con artists at the front door to trusted relatives and caregivers. Nearly every one of these schemes depends on trust the scammer hasn't earned. The scammer poses as someone the victim has reason to believe, such as a romantic partner, a grandchild, a government official, a charity, a contractor or a family helper. Each scheme is larger, more organized or harder to undo than its short description suggests, and each has a defense that can stop it. Reporting is part of that defense, because losses no one reports never reach the official count. Anyone targeted by a romance scammer or a similar con can report it online at ic3.gov, the website of the FBI's Internet Crime Complaint Center. Victims can also call the National Elder Fraud Hotline at 833-372-8311, according to the NIA. Every report brings the official count closer to the true cost.

Romance scammers don't count on carelessness. They count on loneliness. The FBI says fake suitors on dating sites exploit seniors' wish for companionship. That emotional need is an expensive weakness: people 60 and older reported $584 million in losses to romance and confidence scams in 2025, according to HousingWire. That total counts only the money filed under romance scams.

Judged by dollars alone, romance scams are far from the costliest fraud against seniors. People 60 and older lost $3.52 billion to investment fraud in 2025, according to HousingWire. The two categories overlap. A fake suitor met online can steer the conversation toward a crypto or gold "opportunity," and the loss may then be filed as investment fraud even though it began as a courtship. The romance figure likely understates how much older Americans lose through romance-style contact, so its smaller size shouldn't reassure anyone.

Money sent to these scammers rarely comes back. Scammers favor payment methods built for speed, and across all scams aimed at people 60 and older, they collected $257.5 million through crypto ATMs and kiosks in 2025, according to HousingWire. Cash fed into one of those machines can be converted and gone before a victim has second thoughts. Even then the losses may not stop. Fraudsters come back posing as officials or law firms and offer to recover the stolen money for a fee, and seniors lost $540 million to these recovery scams in 2025, according to HousingWire. A romance victim can lose money to the suitor, then to the "investment," then to the fake rescuer, and each loss is harder to reverse than the last.

Older adults can spot this scam before any request for money, at the moment a new contact pushes to leave the site where the two met. The FBI names social media and dating websites as the places where romance scammers find their victims. "The scammers typically initiate contact through text messages, social media sites, advertisements, or dating applications and then quickly move the conversation to a messaging platform," the FBI's Internet Crime Complaint Center said in its annual report, as reported by HousingWire. The defense is to keep the conversation where it started and never send cryptocurrency to someone known only online.

The cheapest time to stop a romance scam is at the first odd request, because after the first payment little money is left to save.

A pop-up warning or a phone call that offers to fix a broken computer is a request for access, not an offer of help. Victims lost over $1.3 billion in 2023 to fake helpers who "fixed" computer problems that did not exist so they could get inside the victims' devices, according to the FBI. Once inside, a scammer can see sensitive information, the kind of details that lead from a computer to a bank account. No one loses a sum that large to bogus repair fees. Losses on that scale come from scammers who go after the savings behind the screen, and the computer is simply their way in.

In the scheme's advanced form, the scammers stop working only through the screen and collect money in person. Scammers who open with a promise to fix a computer take payment in cash, gold, precious metals or wire transfers, the Post and Courier reports, and couriers pick up those payments at victims' homes or at other locations. Cash or gold handed to a stranger is very hard to get back, and a courier sent to collect shows that more than one person is working the victim. That gives older people one firm warning sign. When a computer problem leads to a request to hand cash or gold to a courier, the scam has shown itself, and that is the moment to stop.

Even a single state's losses reach into the millions. South Carolina residents 60 and older told the FBI's Internet Crime Complaint Center that tech support scams took over $9 million from them in the 11 months from January through November, according to the Post and Courier. Those losses came from one state and one age group in less than a year. The scammers want the money, so the first call should go to the bank, not to a repair shop. Anyone who has let a "helper" into a device should contact their financial institutions to protect their accounts.

Shutting the laptop won't stop this scam, but locking down the accounts will.

A grandparent scam caller doesn't need to fool anyone for long, only long enough to get money sent before anyone checks. The FBI describes impostors who claim to be a relative urgently needing money, exploiting family trust. The NIA warns that scammers may threaten people or pressure them to act immediately. In this scheme, that urgency is the main weapon. A grandparent who loves the caller believes the story. A grandparent told to act now feels that stopping to check would mean abandoning a grandchild in trouble. The pressure aims at a grandparent's loyalty rather than their financial judgment, so someone who handles money carefully everywhere else has no special protection here.

The defense is to refuse the deadline. Before sending anything, hang up and talk the request over with someone you trust, such as another family member. A second person ends the private, rushed conversation the scammer needs. A relative who is really in trouble can wait a few minutes while someone checks, but a scammer is usually exposed as soon as someone calls the real grandchild. That is why the caller pushes hardest against any delay.

Shame keeps many victims quiet afterward, because a person fooled through family trust may feel embarrassed in front of that same family. "Don’t be ashamed if you think you or someone you know has been a victim of a scam — it can happen to anyone," the NIA said. Advice from a federal health agency treats falling for the call as something that happens to ordinary people, not as a personal failing. Victims who decide to speak up have several places to start, including local police, the state attorney general and the Federal Trade Commission, according to the NIA.

Telling someone is the one thing this scam is built to prevent, whether before the money is sent or after.

Government impersonation scams work because people fear the government's power to punish them. The FBI includes this scheme among the typical frauds against older people because fake officials threaten arrest or prosecution to frighten victims into paying. The fake official often joins a scam that another criminal has already started, the FBI reports, with a second caller posing as a government official or a bank representative. That second caller's job is to make a fraud already under way look legitimate. A victim who believes they have finally reached someone who can help may be speaking with another member of the same group.

Scammers create panic on purpose to rush victims into payments that are hard to trace or reverse. "Scammers create a sense of urgency to produce fear and lure victims into immediate action," the FBI said. In these schemes, scammers demand cash, gold, precious metals or wires, according to the FBI, and couriers collect the payments using passcodes. The passcode makes the pickup feel like an official procedure rather than a stranger taking someone's savings. Once a courier carries cash or gold away, the victim has no bank transfer to stop and no card charge to dispute.

The type of payment demanded reveals the scam, however convincing the threat sounds. The FBI says that no genuine U.S. government agency asks to be paid with gift cards, in cryptocurrency or precious metals, or by wire transfer. A victim can apply that rule without judging a caller's tone or title. Anyone claiming to be an official who demands gold, wires or gift cards to keep someone out of jail is not acting for any real agency.

A caller can claim any title, but a demand for gold or gift cards is reason enough to hang up.

Scammers open this con with good news about a prize or a worthy cause, and the victim wants to say yes before any doubt sets in. The FBI says these fraudsters pose as charities or promise foreign winnings that can be collected only after paying a fee. People told they have won a contest or lottery then learn they must pay fees, according to the FBI. In every version, the victim has to pay before anything arrives, and nothing ever arrives. The prize exists only to justify the fee. The fake charity appeal works the same way, trading on the trust people give to good works. The payment is still the whole point. A request to pay for something a person has already won is not a step toward the prize. It is the theft itself.

Anyone can receive this pitch, including people who know fraud from the inside. Scammers have aimed it at William Webster, a former director of both the FBI and the CIA. "If it can happen to me, it can happen to you," Webster said. Scammers send the pitch to everyone, so protection can't depend on being too smart to fall for it. It has to be a habit everyone keeps.

A fee paid to collect winnings from abroad is almost certainly gone for good. Ron Miller, a special agent in the FBI's Washington Field Office, says victims are unlikely to get back what they lose to scams, and the odds are worse when the scammers operate overseas. That leaves the moment before payment as the only one that counts. A scammer can demand a fee only from someone who responds, and Miller advises people to ignore the call, letter or email altogether.

Nobody has to pay to collect a real prize, a real charity will wait while a donor checks it out, and hanging up is free.

Home repair con artists skip the inbox and the phone line and knock on the front door with an offer to fix the house. They show up at homes, collect payment up front and never do the work, according to the FBI. The homeowner pays for a promise and loses the deposit itself, with no half-finished job left behind to dispute. Described that way, the scheme sounds like one bad hire and one overpaid bill. It sounds like a local nuisance, not a serious threat to retirement savings.

The stranger on the porch can be the visible member of an organized network that works one community and then moves on to the next. CBC News reports that police in the Waterloo region of Ontario, Canada, say some people tied to these networks come from abroad and travel from town to town, pushing seniors into contracts with misleading sales pitches. A crew that moves between communities can do far more damage than one bad job, and victims in a single investigation in the Waterloo region lost more than $500,000 combined.

Some of these con artists add a badge to the contractor's pitch. People who pose as police officers exploit how readily the public trusts officers, according to remarks by Const. Chris Iden of the Waterloo Regional Police Service reported by CBC News. An older homeowner who faces a uniformed stranger in person on the doorstep has little room to stall or check the story.

Homeowners have one clear defense in how they settle the bill. In the scheme the FBI describes, the con artist takes the homeowner's money before a single repair is made. A homeowner who refuses to hand over the full cost at the start leaves much less for a con artist to take. The safest rule is to pay in stages, and only for work the homeowner can see and check, so a crew that disappears leaves with as little as possible.

A real contractor can wait to be paid for work that is done.

The FBI reports that some scammers reach older Americans indirectly, through television and radio advertising, without ever contacting them directly. The scammers speak to a broad audience at once, so they don't single out anyone by name. Reaching people through a broadcast gives a potential victim fewer reasons to be suspicious. No stranger calls out of the blue, and no unexpected email lands in an inbox to put anyone on guard. Viewers and listeners hear the pitch between ordinary commercials, and nothing marks it as different from the ads around it.

To sound trustworthy, these advertisers attach their pitch to genuine products that many older people have already heard of, such as credit repair or a reverse mortgage. The products themselves are legitimate, and the dishonesty lies in the advertisements. A real product says nothing about whether the person advertising it can be trusted. An older viewer who recognizes a familiar product such as a reverse mortgage may also hear a dishonest offer as routine.

The defense starts by keeping the product and the ad separate. An older viewer can trust that a service is real and still doubt the advertiser selling it, even when the offer sounds familiar and sensible.

The ad deserves the doubt, not the service it names.

Most fraud against older people needs a stranger to earn the victim's trust, but this scheme starts with someone who already has it. The FBI warns that trusted relatives or acquaintances exploit older people and take their money. Regulators treat this as a crime separate from a scam. FinCEN described elder theft as the taking of an older adult's money "by a trusted person," apart from scams that send money "to a stranger or imposter," as reported by the Federal Reserve. The usual advice against fraud is to hang up, check the story and never pay anyone whose identity can't be confirmed. That advice fails when the thief is the person the victim would ask for confirmation. A relative or helper may also have legitimate access to the accounts, so withdrawals that drain the victim can look like routine errands done with permission.

This kind of theft leaves the faintest trail in the official figures. From June 15, 2022, to June 15, 2023, financial institutions sent FinCEN 155,415 filings about suspected exploitation of older people's money, according to the Federal Reserve. Those filings cover theft by trusted people as well as scams by strangers. The FBI, however, gives no loss figure or case count for the family and caregiver scheme. When the person taking the money is also the one the victim relies on to manage it, the victim may have no one else watching the accounts.

The protection that fits an insider threat is a second, independent person who can spot trouble, not more suspicion of strangers. Naming a trusted contact at the bank adds that check without giving anyone new access to the money. A trusted contact can't see the accounts or act on them unless the account holder separately grants more access, according to the Federal Reserve. That makes it safe to choose someone outside the circle of people who already handle the victim's money.

With this scheme, the question to ask is not who is calling but who already has access to the money.

Jensen Huang’s Departure from NVIDIA Sparks Hope of a Gaming Pivot

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Contributor at DualShockers, former Esports Insider DACH content lead, former Escapist Magazine Germany content lead, software engineering background.

NVIDIA has announced that co-founder Jensen Huang has resigned as president, CEO and board member, effective immediately. CFO Colette Kress has been named interim CEO whilst continuing as CFO, with Harvey Jones becoming the board’s first-ever chair. The announcement didn’t give a reason, although it obviously arrived after very public reports of extramarital affairs, one of them reportedly with a woman US intelligence officials suspect of being a Chinese spy. Neither NVIDIA nor Huang has confirmed the reporting, but investors reacted regardless, with shares falling as much as 14% at the open.

Masato Kumzawa addresses fan reactions to Grace Ashcroft's design and the impact of NVIDIA's DLSS 5 AI technology.

Ultimately, I’m not that interested in the gossip. What interests me far more is what happens to a company that has spent the last few years being defined by one man’s bet on AI, and whether that opens the door for the side of the business that first made NVIDIA a household name. For anyone who has tried to buy a graphics card recently, that is a rather important question.

Huang co-founded NVIDIA in 1993 and ran it for more than 33 years, with the move into AI being very much his decision. It has paid off enormously, because data centres now make up roughly 91.5% of the company’s revenue. The flip side is that nobody else has been quite as closely tied to that strategy, which makes for some interesting possibilities for NVIDIA’s future.

Kress has been CFO since 2013 and is a well-respected voice with the market, and eToro’s Josh Gilbert described her as a familiar and trusted presence, whilst adding that a company known for its technical edge might want an engineer in the chair. Analyst Dan Ives expects her stint as interim CEO to last around a year before NVIDIA looks outside. NVIDIA’s company structure is interesting in that it doesn’t make finding a solution any easier: Huang had more than 60 people reporting to him directly.

NVIDIA has no COO, and reports suggest there was no succession plan in place. The internal names analysts have put forward, such as Ian Buck, Bill Dally, Michael Kagan and Bryan Catanzaro, are largely AI and data centre figures, but none of them has been the face of the strategy in the way Huang was. Constellation Research’s Ray Wang pointed out that many of NVIDIA’s partnerships and investments came about because people trusted Jensen.

Which brings me to the hopium. NVIDIA started out making graphics cards for gamers, and CNBC has pointed out that those gamers helped keep the company afloat back in 1999 by buying the GeForce 256. These days they account for under 10% of revenue, and it shows: RTX 50-series production was reportedly cut by up to 40% earlier this year, the Super refresh was reportedly shelved, and the next generation has reportedly drifted towards 2028. Meanwhile, the RTX 5090 launched at $1,999 and third-party sellers have been asking far more than that ever since.

Nvidia DLSS 5 is officially supported in 2K27, but eager fans have found their own way to enable the software in a myriad of titles.

So it isn’t hard to see why some gamers are hoping the board promotes someone with roots on that side of the business. Jeff Fisher, SVP of GeForce, has been at NVIDIA since 1994, but it is obvious that Kress is probably the safer bet for investors. Jonah Alben, who has led GPU engineering since 2008, and co-founder Chris Malachowsky might also throw a hat into this race. It is however important to note that all of this is still very much speculation: nobody at NVIDIA has hinted at a gaming pivot, and whether that will happen with any likeliness will be heavily dependent on other factors.

Truth be told, I wouldn’t expect a new CEO to change much straight away, even one with a soft spot for gaming. The memory shortage squeezing GeForce is affecting the entire industry, with Samsung, SK Hynix and Micron reportedly prioritising AI customers for their high-bandwidth memory, and that won’t disappear because of who is sitting in the corner office.

Whoever takes over will also inherit a company that has just lost its founder and a fair amount of investor confidence, which tends to make boards cautious rather than adventurous. Even so, I’d quite like to see someone in charge who remembers where GeForce came from, and who has some sympathy for the people still trying to buy one. For now, Kress is running both the company and its finances whilst the search for a permanent CEO gets under way.

Between GTA 6's leaked pricing and the Steam Machine's $1,049 reveal, this week made one thing clear: gaming is getting expensive everywhere at once.

SoftBank’s billion-dollar loss may have put a cap on AI’s natural gas ambitions

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SoftBank might not be the first to invest in a hot trend, but when it does, it goes big.

Witness its $30 billion bet on OpenAI in early 2025 or its announcement in February that its SB Energy subsidiary would spend $33 billion on a 9.2-gigawatt natural gas power plant. If you can’t be first, be biggest, I guess.

That philosophy apparently spilled over into procurement, where orders were also supersized. Starting about two years ago, SoftBank reportedly put down deposits on more than 13 gigawatts of turbines, almost 50% more than it needed.

The decision to over-order undoubtedly helped secure turbines and speed up SoftBank’s projects.

“SB Energy did not have a choice except to overbook turbines in the future if they wanted some turbines right away,” Peter Gardett, CEO of Noreva, told TechCrunch via email.

The energy world was caught off guard by the swiftness and magnitude of AI’s rising power demands. Queues for turbine orders now extend into the early 2030s. As natural gas was shaping up to be a go-to power source for the AI boom, CEO Masayoshi Son bet that by deploying his investment firm’s significant war chest, he could corner a portion of the market. If the AI world wanted power for its data centers, it would have to come to him.

Now, that bet appears to have fizzled. SoftBank announced a $7.1 billion quarterly loss on Tuesday, with over half of that stemming from losses related to power-generating equipment. The investor had paid turbine manufacturers nonrefundable deposits to secure orders, and now it considered much of those worthless.

The move was also a costly bit of price discovery, Gardett said. Rather than overpay and potentially still be short of turbines, SoftBank overpaid and over-ordered.

“This charge is just that price discovery finally happening,” Gardett said.

The magnitude of that misplaced bet — over $3 billion — reflects some of the electricity mania that has overtaken the AI world. Faced with years-long waits to connect to the grid, many hyperscalers and developers have turned to natural gas as a sort of energy security blanket, something that is proven and familiar.

As AI data centers spend more time in operation, their operators are getting a better feel for what’s required to keep them operating. Natural gas might seem like a safe bet, but there are still risks, including winter curtailments when supplies dip and demand spikes, Brent Nelson, senior managing director of markets and strategy at Ascend Analytics, told the Equity podcast Wednesday.

In light of that, Nelson said the grid might still be their best bet. Most data centers would be best served by a grid connection, albeit a smaller one than they might normally request. To make up for the shortfall, large batteries are a cost-effective choice, Nelson said. They’d help shave peak loads or ride out requests from the utility to curtail electricity use.

Data center operators might be coming to that same conclusion, leading the natural gas market to develop more slowly than SoftBank expected.

SoftBank’s natural gas-related losses are unlikely to have a domino effect, leading to mass cancellations of gas power plants. AI’s power demand appears to be too large for that. For now, natural gas is riding high, though SoftBank may have found its ceiling.

Just Turn the Heat On? That Might Be Why Your Skin Suddenly Feels So Dry

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

A few weeks ago, I rented a big cabin outside of the city with friends. It was the first cold weekend in New York, the kind where you might actually need to throw on the heavy jackets that were too much for those 55-degree fall days. Nestled in this mountain house, we cozied up around a fire in fleece sweatshirts and fuzzy blankets. Before bed, we turned the heat on. I slept cozy like a baby in the womb, but immediately upon waking, my skin felt tight and dry. I coated myself in moisturizer, but it was no use. My face—which is usually oily and acne-prone—was dull and rough, like it’d never produced a drop of oil in its 29 years.

At first, I thought it was a sign of the times. I’m about to turn 30, and my skin’s just getting drier. But when I headed back to Brooklyn, where my stuffy apartment produces enough natural heat on its own to not need the radiator cranked up, my skin went back to normal.

I’ve heard of this phenomenon before—when your skin gets dehydrated once you turn on the heat in your house—but this was my first experience. And I was determined to get to the bottom of it before my radiator goes on for good and the winter uglies take over. Two dermatologists weighed in—and it looks like this isn’t necessarily preventable but totally treatable.

“Indoor heating lowers the relative humidity in the air, which makes moisture evaporate from your skin more quickly,” says dermatologist Annette Czernik, MD. “Our skin naturally produces oils that help protect it and keep moisture in, but sometimes that's not enough when the air is really dry.” You might apply moisturizer and feel like you didn’t even do anything, she adds.

You’re also not always in the clear if you have oily skin, says Dr. Czernik. “A lot of people don't realize that your skin can be oily and dehydrated at the same time,” she explains. “This is because your skin can still produce oil while losing moisture from the dry air.”

You may also feel tightness, roughness, stinging, flaking, and even itching. “As your skin loses more moisture, its protective barrier can become weaker,” says Dr. Czernik, hence why you can experience some irritation as your skin isn’t as strong enough to fight it off. The air can also make your skin look dull or even feel more sensitive, since it’s working so hard to hold onto the moisture it needs, explains Dr. Czernik. “In more severe cases, you might experience redness, peeling, or even small cracks in your skin,” she adds.

Naturally, with colder air outside, your skin might get drier too, only exacerbating the issue. “When the temperature drops outside, the air usually holds less moisture,” says Dr. Czernik. “Then, when you go indoors, you're exposed to heated air that can also be very dry.” You’re basically losing moisture on both sides, no matter what you do. “Going back and forth between the cold outdoors and warm indoors can make that dryness more noticeable,” she adds. “This is why someone might have perfectly normal skin during the summer but struggle with dryness all winter.”

“When you turn the heat on, the air inside your home can become very dry,” explains dermatologist Ariel Ostad, MD. And one of the easiest ways to add moisture back into the air is a humidifier. “This can help slow down how quickly your skin loses water throughout the day or night,” Dr. Ostad adds.

He recommends getting a bedside humidifier, like the Canopy Bedside Humidifier, for your bedroom and running it while you’re sleeping. “You may notice that your skin feels less tight and dry when you wake up in the morning,” he says. “It can also help with dry lips and irritated skin.” But remember to clean it regularly, as bacteria and mold can build up in your device.

“If your skin starts getting drier, I would first look at the products you're using every day,” says Dr. Ostad. Both Dr. Ostad and Dr. Czernik recommend switching to a thicker moisturizer. Ideally, find one that both moisturizes (meaning it contains ingredients that pull water into your skin, like squalane, hyaluronic acid, panthenol, and glycerin) and seals in hydration (aka ingredients that act as a physical barrier to reduce moisture loss, including ceramides, oils, butters, and silicones). But you could also opt for two moisturizers nightly. I prefer to use a lightweight moisturizer, then follow it up with a barrier cream.

“It's also a good idea to apply your moisturizer right after washing your face or getting out of the shower,” adds Dr. Czernik. If your skin is still damp, even better. This helps pull all that water into your skin (which helps plump it up and decrease dullness), plus it eliminates time for moisture to evaporate from your skin.

Just as hot air can pull water from your skin, so can hot water, explains Dr. Czernik. It doesn’t mean you need to do a daily cold plunge, but opt for mild to warm water temperatures, as scalding water can also increase skin irritation.

Some of the products in your routine could actually be making your dryness worse. Put down all the strong exfoliants, whether it’s your salicylic acid cleanser, an acid-based peel pad, or a retinol serum.

Instead, Dr. Ostad suggests using a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser “that won't strip your skin of its natural oils.” And if you love your exfoliants, you don’t have to ditch them completely, he adds, but you might want to cut back to once or twice per week at most. “You don't need to completely change your skincare routine, but making a few small adjustments can really help during the colder months,” he says.

“If your skin is just a little dry, you can usually manage it at home with a good moisturizer,” says Dr. Ostad. “However, if the dryness continues for a few weeks despite changing your skincare routine, I would recommend seeing a dermatologist.” If you notice your skin becomes extremely itchy or irritated, that’s a good sign to see a doctor too. “Cracking, bleeding, or painful peeling are signs that something more serious could be going on,” adds Dr. Ostad.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing skin, hair, makeup, nail, and fragrance stories.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan, where she covers skincare, makeup, hair, nails, and more across digital and print. She can generally be found in bright eyeshadow furiously typing her latest feature or hemming and hawing about a new product you "have to try." Prior to Cosmopolitan, she wrote and edited beauty content as an Editor at The Everygirl for four years. Follow her on Instagram for makeup selfies and a new hair 'do every few months.

‘The end of Tesla!’: Cybertruck diehards turn on Elon Musk after he pulls the plug

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Tesla’s polarizing pickup is heading to the scrap heap, leaving its biggest online boosters feeling betrayed and questioning Musk’s next big pivot.

Tesla’s polarizing pickup is heading to the scrap heap, leaving its biggest online boosters feeling betrayed and questioning Musk’s next big pivot.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Cybertruck is heading to that shiny cyber-dumpster in the sky, leaving the folks who built online identities around Elon Musk’s polarizing pickup feeling bereft. Usually, Musk’s fanbase treads lightly around their idol’s mercurial decisions. This time, however, the truck’s demise has sparked rare backlash within the Tesla community — aimed squarely at the guy who signed its death warrant.

Troy Meekhof, who posts on numerous social platforms under the moniker “The Cybertruck Guy,” has been vocal about his disappointment in Musk’s decision to wind down Cybertruck production by the end of summer 2027. (The original plan was to start winding down production in the spring, but then Musk decided to extend it a few months.)

In a 33-minute video on YouTube, Meekhof argues that Tesla never fully committed to making the Cybertruck a success, citing a lack of advertising, confusing pricing changes, and the decision to shift focus before the truck could fully establish itself in the market.

“It feels like they didn’t try to make Cybertruck a success,” he said.

He also had choice words for Musk’s reasons for ending the Cybertruck’s short run to make room at Giga Texas for the fully autonomous Cybercab: “Cancelling all of your best products... in favor of an autonomous platform that remains both software and regulatory constrained... seems like an absolutely terrible idea,” Meekhof vented.

The Cybertruck wasn’t the only Tesla product to meet an untimely end — at least according to those with stakes (and often stock options) in the company’s success. Earlier this year, Tesla discontinued the Model S and Model X, two of its flagship vehicles. With the end of the Cybertruck, Tesla is now left with just the Model 3 and Model Y as vehicles you can actually buy. (The Semi is strictly a commercial vehicle, while the Cybercab’s availability as a consumer product remains dubious, at best.)

“Cancelling Cybertruck after all the years of getting it to production to ‘make room for CyberCab’ is a lame excuse,” @TeslaXplored wrote on X. “There are like 500 CyberCabs in one city that only Tesla influencers are riding in. Taking away consumer cars instead of building what the people want will be the end of Tesla!”

He added, “Very sad what happened to Tesla over the last 5 years.”

Meanwhile, at least one prominent aftermarket company is offering to step in and pick up the mantle. Unplugged Performance, which makes aftermarket accessories, said it would produce the Cybertruck and other vehicles on the truck platform under license, with investors funding the effort: “Worth a conversation?”

Musk, for his part, seems less interested in litigating the particulars of Cybertruck production and more intent on selling a vision of Tesla’s future centered around the Cybercab and humanoid robots. “Cybercab will do a majority of all passenger miles in any jurisdiction where it is not prohibited by law,” Musk wrote on X earlier this week. “Obvious to anyone with eyes and a brain.”

He also pushed back on claims that the Cybertruck was killed because it was unprofitable. “The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate,” Musk said. “We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people.”

To be sure, Cybertruck sales have been disappointing over its entire production run. Musk initially theorized that Tesla could sell 250,000-500,000 Cybertrucks annually, but the actual numbers have been far below those projections. Last year, Tesla is estimated to have sold around 20,000 trucks; the company is on track to sell fewer than that this year.

Whether Musk’s autonomous utopia pans out or ends up joining the Cybertruck in the vaporware graveyard remains to be seen. For now, the stainless-steel wedge that was supposed to be the ultimate symbol of an indestructible future has become a monument to Musk’s shifting attention span — and a stark reminder to Tesla’s most loyal boosters that in Elon’s world, nothing should be taken for granted.

Jeep Just Got into the Retro-Gaming Groove with ‘Moab Run’

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The game lets you play as one of seven special-edition Jeeps on Utah's Sand Flats Road.

Modern driving games like Snowrunner or Gran Turismo harness massive computing power to accurately model the physics of piloting a vehicle at speed. Sometimes, however, you just want an overexcited announcer to shout “Awesome!” while you sail a Jeep Gladiator over a crest, misjudge the landing, and impact an immovable sandstone massif at 85 mph.

Such is the appeal of Moab Run, a new retro-themed off-roading game from none other than Jeep itself. Playable on mobile or in a browser window, the game channels the best of 1990s-era 3-D games like Sega Rally or Offroad Challenge, infusing it with playable examples of the very real special-edition Wranglers Jeep has launched over the past year or so.

See all results for new 2027 Jeep Wrangler for sale near 80516

At launch, you can play seven of these Wranglers, from the synthwave-look Rewind to the military cosplay Sarge, with the full dozen coming to future game expansions. There’s also currently just the one course set in Utah at present, but two other tracks will become playable in due course.

Controls are very basic: accelerate, brake, and steer, with an option to powerslide and build up boost for bigger jumps. Jeep includes a somewhat hilarious disclaimer up front: “Do not attempt to recreate these actions in real life,” because, again, 85 mph into sandstone is not so fun in the non-digital world.

As befits a company that’s all about off-roading, the roads in Moab Run are basically just a guideline. You can switch between various overlapping routes, climb sandstone ridges to pick up collectible star points, or just ignore things completely and fly off the road into a canyon river. There are also hidden collectible ducks scattered across the map, because of course there are. It’s a Jeep Thing.

You can’t damage the Wranglers, you seemingly can’t get stuck, and if you purposely drive in the wrong direction, all that’ll happen is a large green arrow will appear to point to where you’re supposed to go. It’s basically a penalty-free way to blow off some steam, and the opposite of what technical real-world off-roading is like.

Also, it’s free. If you fancy getting in a little Jeep Wranglin’ action during your coffee break, Moab Run will do the trick. Go hunt down those hidden ducks, and aim for the hangtime challenge award.
